The companion row of that test pins the other half of the same decision: an id that passes the guard is forwarded **exactly** as Meta wrote it, surrounding spaces and all. Reshaping a support reference is the same class of harm as inventing one | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-22 | `business_discovery` answering `media: { data: null }` would publish `media: null` | `discoverBusiness` keeps an undisclosed media edge ABSENT rather than empty (CC-DATA-2's rule: an own key in `structuredContent` is a positive claim the edge was fetched). The guard is `if (bd.media?.data)`, and only its **truthiness** stands between an explicit null edge and a `media: null` in the result — relaxed to `!== undefined` it publishes the null. That value is outside the declared type (`media?: DiscoveredMedia[]` says absent or array), so a consumer writing `biz.media?.length` — which the type invites — silently reads `undefined` instead of failing. The wire shape is Meta's to change, not ours, and the existing test only covered the `media: {}` wrapper. **Closed 2026-09-02** by pinning the null spelling alongside it. Two `??`→`\|\|` mutants in the same module (`res.data ?? []`, `wire.business_discovery ?? {}`) are documented equivalents, not gaps: the fields are array-or-absent and object-or-absent, so `null` is the only falsy value either can hold and both operators map it to the same place | post-M5 · unit |

## 5. Comments & moderation (CC-COM)

| ID | Scenario | Expected behavior | When / how |
|---|---|---|---|
| CC-COM-1 | Reply to a comment that was just deleted | Graph error → "comment no longer exists"; no retry | M3 · unit |
| CC-COM-2 | Reply to a reply (Instagram supports only one threading level) | Client-side check where detectable; otherwise map Meta's rejection with the one-level rule stated | M3 · docs + unit |
| CC-COM-3 | Commenting on media with comments disabled | Graph rejection mapped; `create_comment` preview warns if `comments_enabled=false` is already known from a prior read | M3 · unit |
| CC-COM-4 | Comment-spam heuristic block (e.g. code 368 temporarily blocked, or 2207051-class) | `kind: upstream`-with-policy-context, never auto-retried; message tells the operator to slow down | M3 · unit |
| CC-COM-5 | Hide/unhide on a comment type that cannot be hidden (e.g. own comment) | **Rules confirmed from docs, no probe needed.** (a) "Comments made by media object owners on their own media objects will always be displayed, even if the comments have been set to `hide=true`" — the call *succeeds* but is a no-op, so the tool must say so rather than report a hidden comment (done — CC-COM-14); (b) "Comments on live video IG Media are not supported" — hide/unhide is unavailable there entirely *[verified 2026-07-30 — IG Comment reference, https://developers.facebook.com/docs/instagram-platform/instagram-graph-api/reference/ig-comment/]* | M3 · unit + docs |
| CC-COM-6 | Empty message, whitespace-only, or > max length reply | zod rejects empty/whitespace client-side. The comment length cap is **undocumented**: neither the IG Comment reference nor the comment-moderation guide states a maximum, and the only official Instagram length caps are for captions (2,200) and DM text (1,000) — different surfaces, do not borrow either *[checked 2026-07-30 — https://developers.facebook.com/docs/instagram-platform/instagram-graph-api/reference/ig-comment/ , https://developers.facebook.com/docs/instagram-platform/comment-moderation/]*. `[verify — needs a live call: POST /{comment-id}/replies with 2,199 / 2,200 / 2,201-character messages and bisect the first rejected length; record code + error_subcode]`. Until then the client-side bound is our own, and Meta's rejection is mapped, not predicted | M3 · unit + live |
| CC-COM-7 | `delete_comment` without `IG_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE` | Refused even with `apply: true` — double gate holds; error names both required flags | M3 · unit |
| CC-COM-8 | `upstream` does not mean "never retried", and a comment said it did | the subcode-2207051 case in `src/core/errors.ts` carried `// spam/integrity restriction — never auto-retried (CC-COM-4)`. The guarantee is not in the kind: `isRetryableKind` (`src/core/http.ts`) answers `idempotent` for `upstream`, so an `upstream` error **is** retried on any idempotent call. 2207051 escapes the retry loop for an unrelated reason — it arises on writes (POST/DELETE), which are never retried at all. This is the dangerous species of wrong comment, because acting on it is reasonable: it invites the next reader either to reuse `upstream` for an integrity failure reachable via GET, or to "simplify" `isRetryableKind` on the belief that the kind already carries non-retryability. Corrected on 2026-09-02 to state the actual mechanism, and the taxonomy table in the file header was corrected to match. The kind itself was left at `upstream`: it is a value other modules key on, and moving it to buy a comment's accuracy is the wrong trade | post-M5 · docs |

## 6. Insights (CC-INS)

| ID | Scenario | Expected behavior | When / how |
|---|---|---|---|
| CC-INS-1 | Account < 100 followers requesting demographics | Meta errors; mapped with the ≥ 100-followers rule named — not a generic permission error | M4 · unit |
| CC-INS-2 | Metric not valid for the media type (story metric on feed post, `navigation` on a reel) | Per-`media_product_type` metric matrix in `api/insights.ts`; invalid combos refused client-side with the valid set listed | M4 · design + unit |
| CC-INS-3 | `since`/`until` older than the 90-day retention | Refused client-side with the retention rule; partially-in-window ranges clamped + flagged in the result | M4 · unit |
| CC-INS-4 | Timezone semantics of `since`/`until` (UTC vs account timezone) | Meta documents only that `since`/`until` take **UNIX timestamps** ("The API will only include data created within this range (inclusive). If you do not include these parameters, the API will look back 24 hours") and that **response** timestamps "use UTC with zero offset … ISO-8601". The timezone the **daily buckets** are cut in is stated nowhere *[checked 2026-07-30 — https://developers.facebook.com/docs/instagram-platform/insights/ , https://developers.facebook.com/docs/instagram-platform/api-reference/instagram-user/insights/]*. `[verify — needs a live call: GET /{ig-id}/insights?metric=reach&period=day&metric_type=time_series with since/until pinned to an exact UTC midnight boundary; compare the returned bucket end_time values against UTC midnight and against the account's local midnight]`. Whichever it is gets documented in the tool description so the model formats correctly | M4 · live + docs |
| CC-INS-5 | Insights on media created before the account became professional | Meta returns empty/error; mapped with that explanation | M4 · docs |
| CC-INS-6 | `online_followers` disappears (it is on the deprecation watch-list) | Tool degrades to an explicit "metric no longer available" error; watch item in the version-upgrade checklist | M4 · docs |
| CC-INS-7 | Legacy metric names (`impressions`, `profile_views`) requested by the model out of habit | Input enum only contains the post-2025 set; the error for unknown metrics lists valid ones — the model self-corrects | M4 · unit |
| CC-INS-8 | An `until`-only window that ends before the 90-day floor was forwarded to Graph instead of refused | **Found and fixed by mutation testing 2026-09-02.** CC-INS-3's refusal in `getAccountInsights` read `since !== undefined && until !== undefined && until < floor`, so it could only fire on a window with BOTH endpoints. `since` and `until` are independently `.optional()` in the tool's zod schema, so the ordinary "everything up to `<date>`" shape slipped past it: the call went out, Graph answered `data: []`, and the operator got a result indistinguishable from a genuinely idle account — having spent a rate-limited call to get it. It also contradicted the tool's own description, which promises that a partially-old range is clamped and flagged while a fully-old one is rejected. The fix keys the refusal on the window's **end** alone (`until !== undefined && until < floor`), which is the only endpoint that decides whether retained data can exist at all. Side effect worth recording: it converted a previously-equivalent mutant (dropping the `\|\| until !== undefined` arm of the outer guard) into a killed one — the guard now has one reason instead of two. Pinned in both directions, including a `since` sitting exactly on the floor, so the `<` cannot drift to `<=` | post-M5 · unit |

## 7. Config, profiles & env (CC-CFG)

| ID | Scenario | Expected behavior | When / how |
|---|---|---|---|
| CC-CFG-1 | `account` argument names an undefined profile | Error lists the configured profile names (names only — never token values) | M1 · unit |
| CC-CFG-2 | Mixed auth paths across profiles (default = Path A, `IG_PROFILE_BRAND_*` = Path B) | Supported: auth mode resolved **per profile**; capability differences follow the profile's path | M1 · design + unit |
| CC-CFG-3 | Env file present but world-readable (not `0600`) | Startup warning (not fatal) naming the file and the fix; server-written files always get `0600` | M1 · unit |
| CC-CFG-4 | CRLF line endings / quoted values / `=` inside token values in the env file | dotenv semantics tested explicitly; atomic rewrite preserves comments and does not mangle quoting | M2 · unit |
| CC-CFG-5 | `IG_ENV_FILE` points to a nonexistent path | Clear startup error (explicit intent), while a missing *default* XDG file is fine (env-only config) | M1 · unit |
| CC-CFG-6 | Unknown tool arguments (model hallucinates a param) | `.strict()` zod → validation error naming the unknown key and the valid keys | M1 · unit |
| CC-CFG-7 | Package profile math: `IG_TOOL_PACKAGES=all` + `IG_PACKAGES_DENY=discovery` + `IG_PACKAGES_READONLY=publishing` | Deterministic resolution order (profile → deny → readonly), snapshot-tested; denied packages produce **no network calls** ever | M1 · unit |
| CC-CFG-8 | Windows: no XDG — config/state paths and `0600` semantics undefined | Per-platform path resolution (`%APPDATA%\instagram-mcp-ai\`); `chmod` skipped on win32; covered by the CI Windows leg | M1 · unit |
| CC-CFG-9 | The same key assigned twice in the env file, and `login` rewrites it | **Closed 2026-09-01.** A duplicate is ordinary in a hand-edited file: an operator pastes a new `IG_ACCESS_TOKEN=` line at the bottom rather than editing the one already there. The merge used to consume each update as it applied it, so it rewrote the FIRST occurrence and left the later one holding the stale value — and dotenv is last-one-wins, so the reader resolved exactly that. Measured outcome: a revoked token both stayed on disk in cleartext and BEAT the fresh one written above it, so `login` reported success while every subsequent call authenticated with the dead credential. `mergeEnv` now rewrites every occurrence of a key it recognises, and appends the keys it did not find AFTER the existing content — so if some assignment survives in a shape the line parser does not recognise, the value written last is still the one a last-one-wins reader picks. The rewrite is now idempotent on a file it has already repaired. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-10 | The config directory already exists at a looser mode than `0700` | **Closed 2026-09-01.** `mkdir(dir, { recursive: true, mode: 0o700 })` applies the mode only to directories it CREATES, so a store that predates the rule — or one an operator recreated by hand, `chmod -R`-ed, or restored from an archive that dropped modes — kept whatever it had. `0600` on the file does not cover this: write permission on the DIRECTORY is what lets another local account rename our env file away and leave its own in place, after which the next `login` writes a fresh token into a file somebody else controls. `atomicWrite` now issues an unconditional POSIX `chmod(dir, 0o700)` after the `mkdir` — unconditional rather than stat-then-chmod, because the wanted mode is a constant and the check would only add a race with the same outcome. **Accepted cost:** if the directory belongs to another user the chmod raises `EPERM` and `login` now fails loudly where it used to succeed. That is the intended trade: a store we cannot secure is one we must not quietly fill with credentials. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-11 | A relative `configDir` resolves against the process CWD, so `login` deposits credentials wherever it was run | Measured, **not fixed — owner decision.** The path is joined without being resolved against the home base, so a relative `configDir` writes a real `.env` under whatever directory the operator happened to be standing in. This is not hypothetical: a `0600` file with `IG_ACCESS_TOKEN` and `IG_AUTH_PATH` sits in this repository root from exactly such a run (gitignored, so it never reached history — but only because `.env` happens to be ignored). The candidate fixes each cost something real: rejecting relative paths breaks any operator who deliberately uses one, and resolving them against the config home silently retargets a path the operator spelled out. Both change a documented CLI contract, so the call is the owner’s. **Corrected 2026-09-23:** `login` has no `--config-dir` flag (`src/cli/login.ts`); a relative `configDir` is reachable only through the programmatic `writeCredentials` option, and a relative `XDG_CONFIG_HOME`/`APPDATA` is ignored rather than resolved against the CWD. The CLI exposure this row describes does not exist today. | post-M5 · observed |
| CC-CFG-12 | Env-file write durability and failure surface: no `fsync`, an orphaned `.tmp` on a failed rename, a symlinked target replaced by the rename, and raw `node:fs` errors reaching the operator | Measured, **deliberately not fixed.** `rename` is atomic with respect to readers but not durable across a power loss, so a crash in the wrong millisecond can leave a truncated store; an `fsync` on the file and its directory would close that, at the cost of a real disk barrier on every `login`. A rename that fails leaves the randomly-named `.tmp` sibling behind — `0600`, so not a disclosure, but litter. Writing through a symlinked env file replaces the LINK rather than its target, which surprises anyone who symlinks the store into a dotfiles repo. And a failing write surfaces the raw `ENOSPC`/`EACCES` from `node:fs` rather than an `InstagramError` naming the path and the remedy. All four are real; none can corrupt or disclose a credential on a successful write, which is why they rank below the two fixes above rather than beside them. **The error surface was fixed 2026-09-19; the other three remain deliberately unfixed.** A single `storeFailure(err, verb, filePath)` helper wraps every `node:fs` rejection from the credential store in `InstagramError({ kind: 'validation', cause: err })`, with the message "Cannot read/write the credential store at <path> (<code>): <remedy>". It names the file the operator knows about — never the random `.tmp` sibling, which is what made `ENOSPC: no space left on device, open '/…/.env.3f9a2c.tmp'` unreadable — plus the `code`, the only part of the raw error worth carrying since `ENOSPC` vs `EACCES` is the diagnosis, and the remedy for that side; the raw error rides along as `cause`, so a log loses nothing. The two remedies live in a `STORE_REMEDY` lookup rather than a conditional so the helper has no branch of its own, and `code` is interpolated with no `?? 'unknown'` fallback, which would be a branch no test can take. `readExisting` still treats ENOENT as an empty store and now wraps everything else (`EISDIR`, `ENOTDIR`, `EACCES`, `ELOOP`) rather than letting it pass as "no store yet", which would replace a store that was never read; `atomicWrite` wraps the whole five-call sequence in one `try`, so whichever step fails the operator is told about the store rather than about the step. `kind: 'validation'` is the kind this repo already gives a local-environment failure carrying a raw `cause` (the OAuth redirect listener in `cli/login.ts`): nothing upstream was involved and `core/http.ts` retries only `rate_limit`/`upstream`. Six tests in `test/core/config-write.test.ts`: `a read failure other than ENOENT is wrapped, naming the store, the code and the remedy`, `an unreadable existing file aborts the write instead of being replaced`, `a write failure is wrapped, naming the store, the code and the remedy, with no temp sibling`, `a directory the user cannot create the store under is a wrapped EACCES`, `ENOENT on read still means an empty store, so a first login creates the file`, and `a failure at the temp write, after the directory calls, is wrapped exactly as one at mkdir`. Unchanged and still accepted: no `fsync`, the orphaned `.tmp` after a failed rename (0600, so litter rather than disclosure), and the symlinked target replaced by the rename. Mutation-verified 2026-09-22 with twelve hand mutants over the two wrappers — the read-side wrapper removed (the pre-fix `throw err;`), the ENOENT test inverted, every read failure swallowed as "no store yet", the read side given the write verb and remedy, the write-side wrapper removed, the raw error no longer carried as `cause`, the kind changed to `upstream`, the errno dropped from the message, the two remedies swapped, the first fs call left outside the `try`, and the message naming the temp sibling instead of the store — eleven killed outright. The twelfth was a real gap, and is why the sixth test exists: a mutant that wraps only `mkdir` and `chmod(dir)` and leaves `writeFile(tmp)` → `chmod(tmp)` → `rename` raw was invisible, because every write-side test until then staged its failure at the FIRST fs call. `a failure at the temp write, after the directory calls, is wrapped exactly as one at mkdir` reaches the second half with no mocking of `node:fs` and no privileges: it builds a store path whose directory is creatable but whose file name pushes the full path past POSIX `PATH_MAX`, so `mkdir` and `chmod` succeed and the `open` of the temp sibling fails `ENAMETOOLONG`; the limit is bisected with `readFile` probes rather than hard-coded per platform, the whole message is asserted along with `cause.code` and `cause.syscall`, and the store’s directory is checked to be empty afterwards, which is the no-temp-sibling half. Windows is skipped, the lever being a POSIX limit. `rename` failing after a successful temp write (EXDEV, a locked file) sits inside the same `try` and is deliberately not staged on its own — the strengthening test already proves the second half of the sequence is wrapped **Durability reversed 2026-09-23 (CC-CFG-54):** the temp file is now `fsync`ed before the rename and the directory flushed after it; the orphaned `.tmp` was already closed by CC-CFG-48. Only the symlinked-store behaviour remains deliberately unfixed. **Symlink half reversed 2026-09-24 (CC-CFG-68):** a write now follows a symlinked store to its target, and nothing in this row is left unfixed | post-M5 · observed |
| CC-CFG-13 | Booleans are case-insensitive, enums are not, and a mistyped `IG_*` name is not a startup error but a silent default | `loadSettings` lower-cases before comparing for boolean knobs, so `IG_ALLOW_DESTRUCTIVE=TRUE`, `True` and `true` are one value; enum knobs are matched exactly, so `IG_TRANSPORT=HTTP` is refused at startup while `IG_TRANSPORT=http` is accepted. Two spellings of one convention in a single parser — and the refusal is the *harmless* half, because the operator at least gets a message naming the variable. The quiet half is that nothing detects an `IG_*` name the server does not recognise at all: `IG_WRITEMODE=apply`, or any other transposition of `IG_WRITE_MODE`, is ignored without a word and the server runs in the default `preview`, so a write knob the operator believes they set is not set. The check cannot live in `settings.ts` alone — `core/config.ts` owns the `IG_PROFILE_*` family and `mcp/registry.ts` owns the profile and tool-selection knobs, so no single module knows the whole recognised set. Recommendation on the table: make enum parsing case-insensitive to match the booleans, and add a startup **warning** (not a refusal) listing unrecognised `IG_*` names, assembled at the composition root where all three owners are in scope. **Fixed 2026-09-19.** Both halves applied. `parseEnumEnv` lower-cases before comparing, exactly like the booleans, and hands back the canonical spelling — `IG_TRANSPORT=HTTP`, `Http`, ` http ` are one value; the refusal message still echoes the raw text (`got "Ws"`). `IG_AUTH_MODE` folds the same way (`IG-LOGIN` → `ig-login`), which matters because every downstream comparison is against the *resolved* `profile.authPath`. The warning is assembled in `src/index.ts` from a published half per owner: `SETTINGS_ENV_NAMES` (`core/settings.ts`), `isProfileEnvName` (`core/config.ts` — the six bare fields, the `IG_PROFILE_<NAME>_*` family under the same length-and-name rule `readNamedRaw` applies, `IG_ACTIVE_PROFILE`, and the `TOKEN_EXPIRES_AT` metadata `login`/`refresh` write into the operator's `.env`, without which every refreshed install would warn about the server's own handwriting), `PACKAGE_ENV_NAMES` (`mcp/registry.ts`) and the entry's own `IG_ENV_FILE`/`IG_HTTP_TOKEN`. Each half is pinned against what its owner *actually reads* by a recording `Proxy` over `process.env` (`SETTINGS_ENV_NAMES is exactly the IG_* set loadSettings reads`, `PACKAGE_ENV_NAMES is exactly the IG_* set selectPackages reads`, `isProfileEnvName vouches for exactly the keys the profile scheme reads or writes`), so a knob added without a list entry fails its own suite instead of being reported to operators as a typo. It is a `warn` record — names only, sorted, never a value, because a mistyped profile key carries a live token — and it is logged BEFORE `loadProfiles()`, so the operator whose misspelt token key stops the start still sees it; `createRedactor` reads the registry live, so building the logger first costs nothing. Two entry tests pin it end to end: `an IG_* name nothing reads is warned about by name at startup, values withheld` (`doctor` still exits 0, exactly one record, `names` exactly the three typos, the value absent, `IG_TOKEN_EXPIRES_AT` absent) and `the unrecognised-name warning reaches the operator whose typo stops the start` (warn line first, then the unchanged byte-for-byte refusal). The test harness's own `IG_TEST_*` knobs are deleted by the preload once read, so production code never learns the harness exists. Writing the first test surfaced CC-PROC-72: the token-shape backstop was masking the very names the warning prints | post-M5 · design |
| CC-CFG-14 | `127.0.0.01` is loopback, `127.0.0.0001` is not, and the comment explaining the line draws the boundary somewhere else | `isLoopbackHost`'s IPv4 check accepts each octet at one to three digits, so the padded spelling `127.0.0.01` clears the digit shape, the four-octet length check and the `127.` prefix, and the server binds it as loopback — which is to say without requiring `IG_HTTP_TOKEN`. `127.0.0.0001`, at four digits, is refused. That refusal was justified in `test/core/settings.test.ts` by "libc would read the leading zero as octal", which is equally true of the three-digit padded spellings the check *accepts*: the comment argued for a rule the code does not implement. **Comment corrected 2026-09-02** — it now states the octet-length rule and says outright that the boundary is about spelling, not range. Neither spelling is a hole today — every value that passes is still inside `127.0.0.0/8`, which is what the bind decision actually turns on, and `inet_aton` reading `127.0.0.01` as octal still lands on 127.0.0.1. What is at stake is smaller and more durable: the stated reason cannot be used to predict what the function does, so the next person to extend it will extend the wrong rule. Recommendation on the table: either tighten the shape to canonical octets with no leading zeros, or restate the comment as a spelling rule rather than an octal one — the first is the smaller lie. Adjacent drift found in the same pass: `writeJournal` is documented in `src/core/types.ts` as an "Absolute path", but a relative `IG_WRITE_JOURNAL` passes through verbatim and is resolved against the process CWD at append time, the same shape as CC-CFG-11's relative `configDir` | post-M5 · design |
| CC-CFG-37 | `test/env-catalog.test.ts` checks that architecture §12 states the true accepted range of every numeric knob, by looping over a hand-written `NUMERIC_KNOBS` list. That list is the one in the file without a counterpart, and the test is a per-item loop over it: a knob the list omits is a knob whose documented range nothing checks, and the loop walks past it reporting the knobs it does name as healthy. Adding a sixth clamp to `loadSettings` is the ordinary way that happens — nothing about writing one prompts an edit there. The document's side was unguarded too: a `(range ...)` note on a row for something `loadSettings` does not clamp promises an operator a validation that will not happen. | **Closed 2026-09-23** with `every knob loadSettings clamps has a documented range, and no other row claims one`. The clamped set is scraped out of `src/core/settings.ts` and compared to `NUMERIC_KNOBS` by a two-directional `deepEqual`, which also floors the scrape — an empty scrape mismatches. The §12 table is sliced out of the document by its heading and its `(range ...)` notes counted against the scraped clamps, so a note on a non-knob row is reported. The bounds themselves are deliberately not scraped: the file's stated design is that the bounds are read out of the docs and enforced against the code, so the table cannot be the thing that is wrong. Measured, each alone: dropping `IG_PORT` from `NUMERIC_KNOBS` survived 4 of 4 tests before the repair and is killed after it; an extra `(range 1–4)` note on the `IG_LOG_LEVEL` row does the same. | post-M5 · unit |

## 8. Process, transports & MCP layer (CC-PROC)

> Also the catalogue's chronological tail: every row added after the original
> sectioning lands here regardless of prefix. See the preamble.

| ID | Scenario | Expected behavior | When / how |
|---|---|---|---|
| CC-PROC-1 | Anything writes to stdout in stdio mode (stray `console.log`) | Protocol corruption — banned by lint rule (`no-console`) + a test asserting the logger targets stderr only | M0 · unit |
| CC-PROC-2 | Client disconnects mid-request (long poll, big fetchAll) | AbortSignal wired through `IgRequestFn` — `IgRequestOptions.signal` (`src/core/types.ts`, frozen contract) reaches `fetch` combined with the per-attempt timeout via `AbortSignal.any([opts.signal, timeout])`, and the same signal is passed to every backoff `sleep` so a disconnect during a retry wait aborts too; in-flight Graph calls aborted, no orphaned timers. **Symbol name corrected 2026-09-01** — this row cited a non-existent `igRequest`; the exported names are `IgRequestFn` and `createIgRequest` | M1 · unit |
| CC-PROC-3 | SIGINT/SIGTERM between container create and publish | On the HTTP transport the first signal runs the transport's `close()` — in-flight exchanges, then every socket, then the listener — bounded at 10 s, and exits 0 (1 if the close fails or times out; a second signal exits at once with 128 + signo). stdio installs no handler: its clean stop is stdin EOF, and a signal ends it by Node's default. Neither transport waits for a tool handler still running, and a composite post is journaled only once its flow returns, so a signal between create and publish leaves no journal line; the granular `instagram_create_media_container` journals the container id at creation (see CC-PUB-16, corrected 2026-09-23) | M2 · unit |
| CC-PROC-4 | HTTP transport: port in use, or `IG_HTTP_TOKEN` unset | Port conflict → clear startup error. **Corrected 2026-09-02** — this row said a missing bearer token refuses to start the HTTP transport outright, and that has never been what ships. That correction described a rule graded by what the bind exposes: a token buys a non-loopback bind, and a blank token is refused in both cases. **Corrected again 2026-09-23** ([[CC-PROC-175]]) — neither half survived measurement, and each was wrong in the direction that costs the operator something. `IG_HTTP_HOST` is validated in `core/settings.ts` before the transport is built, and that check never reads the token, so **every** non-loopback spelling is refused outright, bearer or not. A **blank** token (`IG_HTTP_TOKEN=` or whitespace, which is what an unset shell variable or an empty unit-file line produces) is trimmed away at the entry point and counts as no token: the server starts, serves anonymous requests, and logs the same **error**-level no-authentication line an unset token produces. What ships is the flat rule — loopback only, and a blank token is no token said out loud. `startHttp` still carries the graded guard, which is now defence in depth for a direct caller of that function; no `IG_HTTP_HOST` an operator can set reaches it. See [security.md](security.md) §3 | M1 · unit |
| CC-PROC-5 | Two server instances appending to the write journal concurrently | Append-only writes with `O_APPEND` single-line JSON records — interleaving-safe by construction | M2 · design + unit |
| CC-PROC-6 | Tool result larger than the client can render | Character-budget halving loop guarantees a bounded payload; `truncated: true` in structured content | M1 · unit |

| CC-PROC-7 | A model-supplied object id containing `#`, `?`, `&` or `../` is interpolated into the Graph request path | `buildUrl` concatenates `https://<host>/v25.0` + `path` literally, and every api module except `account.ts` interpolated ids **unencoded**. Verified against the WHATWG URL parser: `#` moves the whole query — **including `access_token`** — into the fragment and the request goes out **unauthenticated**; `?`/`&` inject parameters *ahead* of the auth parameters `http.ts` merges in; `../` normalises `/v25.0/../me` to `/me` and the **version pin silently disappears**. The host cannot be changed this way and the scheme is a proven literal, so this is path/query injection, not SSRF. Expected: encode every interpolated id at the api layer **and** constrain the id charset at the tool layer — either alone is one bug away from regressing. **Closed 2026-08-28** at both layers: 21 `encodeURIComponent` call sites across the five api modules, and `src/tools/ids.ts` (`/^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}$/`) on 16 id fields. `.` is excluded deliberately — one `..` segment eats the version pin. `after`, `hashtag` and `username` keep their own rules and are pinned as *not* carrying the id rule: the first two are query-borne and `URLSearchParams`-encoded, and `username` goes into a Graph field expression where percent-encoding is not an escape. A later finding sharpened the claim: `encodeURIComponent` alone is **not** sufficient — an id of exactly `.` or `..` survives it unchanged and still becomes a dot segment, so a correctly-encoded call site can address a different object (`/v25.0/./children`) or lose the version pin. The tool-layer charset blocks that, and since 2026-08-28 so does the `buildUrl` invariant. **Residual closed 2026-08-28** — see CC-PROC-15. **Coverage re-measured 2026-09-01, and the sweep’s own completeness claim was wrong**: it reached five api modules (20 call sites, not 21) and missed `api/discovery.ts` entirely, while at the tool layer `instagram_get_hashtag_media`’s `hashtagId` was still a plain `z.string().min(1)` rather than the id charset. That one tool was therefore the case this row exists to prevent — **neither** layer holding — and a model-chosen `hashtagId` went into the path unencoded and unconstrained. Now 22 api call sites across **six** modules and 16 tool-layer id fields. The lesson is about the audit, not the code: a sweep that counts the call sites it changed cannot see the module it never opened, so the number was re-derived from the tree rather than from the previous note | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-8 | A tool declares `annotations.readOnlyHint: true` but its handler writes | `registry.ts`'s `if (forceReadonly && spec.annotations.readOnlyHint !== true) continue;` is the *only* thing keeping write tools off a forced read-only surface (`IG_TOOL_PACKAGES=reader`, `IG_PACKAGES_READONLY`), yet nothing binds the annotation to the handler's real behaviour — the write gate is invoked by the handler, not by the registry. Such a tool would be both advertised to the client as safe and pass the filter. `ToolSpec` is a frozen contract, so the invariant is enforced by a contract test rather than by the type system. **Closed 2026-08-27** by `test/mcp/tool-metadata-contract.test.ts`, which *executes* all 28 handlers against an instrumented gate context and derives read-vs-write from observed behaviour (not from a list of names), under four `writeMode` × `allowDestructive` configurations that must all agree; a probe that cannot be classified fails the test rather than defaulting to "read". Current state: 17 read / 11 write, all three axes agreeing | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-9 | A tool's `package` tag does not match the module it is defined in | The registry groups by tag, so the env-var controls follow the **tag**, not the module. `instagram_set_comments_enabled` is a write tool defined in `src/tools/comments.ts` but tagged `media`: `IG_PACKAGES_DENY=comments` does not exclude it and `IG_PACKAGES_READONLY=comments` does not make it read-only. Expected: the mapping is pinned by test so any change to it is deliberate; whether to move the tag is an owner decision because it changes the published surface and the profile counts. **Consequence measured 2026-08-27:** an operator who asks for "no comment mutations" via `IG_PACKAGES_DENY=comments` loses the 8 comments tools but **keeps** the toggle; the `media` spellings do reach it, but `IG_PACKAGES_DENY=media` also removes the two genuine media reads — so **no incantation isolates just this write**. `IG_TOOL_PACKAGES=reader` does drop it, via `READONLY_PROFILES` plus the absent `readOnlyHint: true`, and `IG_PACKAGES_READONLY=media` is the only read-only control that neutralises it. **Open — owner's decision** | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-10 | Caller-supplied log fields collide with the built-in record keys | `{ level, msg, time, ...extra }` spreads caller fields **last**, so `log.info('ok', { level: 'debug', msg: '…', time: 0 })` overwrites all three built-ins — log forging by a tool argument that reaches a log field. Now pinned by a killed mutant, so reversing the order is a test-visible decision rather than an accident. **Closed 2026-08-30 — reserved keys win, and the collision is preserved rather than dropped.** Measured first: `log.info('real message', { level: 'debug', msg: 'forged', time: 0, keep: 'me' })` emitted `{"level":"debug","msg":"forged","time":0,"keep":"me"}`, and `log.child({ msg: 'binding-forged' }).error('real')` forged it through a *binding* too — a child binding is as caller-derived as a per-call field. The built-ins are now written first and each caller key is copied in one at a time; a name in `{level, msg, time}` is re-keyed to `fields.level` / `fields.msg` / `fields.time`. Chosen over the simpler "built-ins last" because that silently **drops** the caller's value, hiding information from the same operator the reserved keys exist to protect. Emitted key order for non-colliding fields is byte-identical to before. Accepted trade-off with its own test: a caller passing both `msg` and a literal `fields.msg` collapses them into one slot — both values are caller-supplied, so the operator-facing `msg` is still unforgeable | post-M5 · design |
| CC-PROC-11 | `createLogger` is called with a level outside the vocabulary | `LEVEL_WEIGHT[level]` yields `undefined`, `undefined < undefined` is `false`, so the filter is disabled and **every** record emits, `debug` included. `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` does not catch it because the map is keyed by the `LogLevel` union rather than by `string`. `settings.ts` enum-validates today, so only a direct caller using a cast is exposed. **Closed 2026-08-30.** Measured: `level: 'verbose'` emitted **all four** levels including `debug`, and `level: undefined` behaved identically. `createLogger` now validates at the boundary (`typeof value === 'string' && Object.hasOwn(LEVEL_WEIGHT, value)`) and an unknown level resolves to `info` — the same default `settings.ts` applies to `IG_LOG_LEVEL`, so a misconfiguration lands where the operator already expects to be. It is announced rather than silent: the logger writes one `warn` record carrying `requestedLevel` and `effectiveLevel`, with a non-string request rendered as `[unprintable]` instead of raw. Deliberately does **not** throw — a logging misconfiguration must not take the server down. `emit`'s own `LEVEL_WEIGHT[level]` lookup is left total: the four adapters only ever pass literals, and the hole was always at the constructor | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-12 | A log field is not JSON-serialisable (e.g. a `BigInt`) | `JSON.stringify` throws out of `emit` and into the calling request path — a logging concern becomes a request failure. Cycles survive only because the *injected* redactor returns `[Circular]`; `log.ts` itself has no cycle defence. **Correction (2026-08-30): the original wording understated this.** It implied the injected redactor was a general defence; it is not. `redactValue` returns any non-string, non-object value unchanged, so **a `BigInt` threw even with the real `createRedactor()` injected** — production was exposed through the ordinary path, not only via an embedder swapping the redactor. Measured: `TypeError: Do not know how to serialize a BigInt`, **zero bytes written**; a throwing `toJSON` propagated verbatim. **Closed 2026-08-30**: serialization is wrapped and degrades to `{level, msg, time, logError, logErrorDetail}` instead of propagating. The degraded record is built from *coerced* values, so it is provably serialisable — otherwise a non-string `msg`, or a clock returning a `BigInt`, would throw again from inside the `catch`. `logErrorDetail` goes through the same injected redactor as `msg`, and a non-`Error` rejection from a hostile `toJSON` is reported generically because `String(aSymbol)` itself throws. Cycle-tolerant serialization was deliberately *not* added — it would duplicate the redactor; a cycle with no redactor now degrades instead of throwing, and with one still renders `[Circular]` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-13 | Tool order changes in `tools/list` | The order the MCP client sees — and therefore the order the model weighs — is the barrel's array order. Before 2026-08-26 nothing pinned the order *within* a package, and the only guard on cross-package order was `docs-sync.test.ts`, whose failure message told the maintainer to regenerate the README **from** the scrambled surface. Now pinned explicitly by `test/tools/index.test.ts` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-14 | `readOnlyHint` is declared inconsistently across the write tools | The 5 publishing writes declare `readOnlyHint: false`; the 6 comments writes omit the field entirely. The registry filter tests `!== true` and MCP defaults the hint to `false`, so behaviour is identical — but the client-facing surface is not, and "hint absent" is a weaker review signal than "explicit `false`" when a new tool is added. Expected: state it explicitly on every write, or state nowhere and rely on the default; not both. **Closed 2026-08-29 — stated explicitly.** The count was checked before the fix and the whole surface swept for a fourth inconsistent module: `account` (3), `discovery` (3), `insights` (4, through a shared `readOnly` constant) and `media` (2) all declare `readOnlyHint: true`, so `comments` was the only gap. The declaration is now **enforced rather than conventional** — `test/mcp/tool-metadata-contract.test.ts` already derives read-vs-write by executing every handler, and a new assertion fails any tool whose `readOnlyHint` is absent, naming it. Proven to bite: dropping the field from `instagram_delete_comment` produces exactly one failure, while `test/tools/comments.test.ts` stays 39/39 green — the existing tests were blind to the difference | post-M5 · design |
| CC-PROC-15 | A future api function interpolates an id into `path` and forgets `encodeURIComponent` | CC-PROC-7 was fixed call site by call site; `buildUrl` itself still accepts a path containing `#`, `?` or `../` without objection, so the class of defect is closed only for the code that exists today. Two candidate guards, neither applied: an **invariant** assert in `buildUrl` that the built URL still begins with `/{GRAPH_VERSION}/` and carries no fragment (an invariant, not encoding — it cannot double-encode `account.ts:56`), and a drift test making `PATH_CALLS` in `test/api/path-injection.test.ts` provably exhaustive rather than exhaustive-by-docstring. **Closed 2026-08-28 — both guards applied.** The invariant is not the naive `hash !== '' \|\| search !== ''`: the URL parser returns `''` for an *empty* component too, so `…/v25.0/X#` passes that test and then `buildUrl` appends `?…&access_token=…` behind the `#` — the naive form misses precisely the leak it exists for. The check is structural equality instead — `` `${parsed.origin}${parsed.pathname}` `` must reassemble the base byte for byte — which covers `#`, `?` and dot segments at any position plus anything else the parser would rewrite (`\`, spaces, control characters). It compares and never rewrites, so double-encoding `account.ts:56` is impossible by construction, and that is pinned by a byte-for-byte test over the full `encodeURIComponent` alphabet. The inventory turned out to have been wrong from the start: it exercised **8 of the 22** api functions that build a computed path, so the old docstring's grep-exhaustiveness claim was never true — a guard that declared coverage it did not have | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-16 | A Graph object id longer than 64 characters, or containing `.` or `:`, ships one day | `GRAPH_ID_PATTERN` refuses it with no env flag to loosen the rule — only a new release. The longest id observed in the wild is 35 characters and the bound is deliberately wider than that, but the id space belongs to Meta. Recorded because an over-strict rule that refuses a real id is a worse failure than the injection it guards against, and this is the way the CC-PROC-7 fix would itself do harm | post-M5 · live |
| CC-PROC-17 | An Instagram error message reaches the model inside a tool result | `errorResult` in `src/mcp/result.ts` carries a docstring promising that a downstream layer — named as "`mcp/redact.ts`" — masks anything sensitive. **That module does not exist.** The real redactor is `src/core/redact.ts` and it is wired into the **log** sinks only; the tool-result body (`Instagram error (kind): message`) reaches the client without passing through it. The exposure is bounded today because `src/core/errors.ts` builds the message from the Graph error object and never embeds the request URL, which is where `access_token` lives — so the promise is unmet rather than the control breached. Expected: either route the result body through the redactor or delete the claim; a comment describing a control that was never built is worse than no comment. **Closed 2026-08-29 — the control was built, and the audit found the real hole was somewhere else.** All **47** `new InstagramError(` sites reachable by `errorResult` were traced: none embeds a URL, header, request body or token value, so there was no active leak through *errors* — the property was held by a convention spread across `errors.ts`, every `api/` projection and `buildUrl`, which is precisely the "depends on one function never changing" shape that §2 of [security.md](security.md) described as an *enforced control*. The genuine gap was the **success** path: `text()` sends an arbitrary string, `json()` serialises a payload, and `structuredContent` was attached **by reference** to the handler's own object — all of it reaching the client unredacted. Fixed in `src/mcp/registry.ts`, not in `result.ts`: the builders are a convention, not a boundary (a handler can return a result literal and never call them), `result.ts` is declared dependency-free with a Gate-G1-frozen four-function API and 44 call sites, and `registry.ts` already had the right precedent (`deps.redact ?? createRedactor()`). `redactResult` wraps `registerOne`'s callback — the one path a result takes to the client — **fails closed** (a redactor that returns a malformed result withholds the raw one) and deep-copies, so `structuredContent` is no longer live | post-M5 · design |
| CC-PROC-18 | — (no operator scenario; static defect) | `loadEnvFiles` in `src/index.ts` guarded with `explicit && explicit !== ''`, but `''` is falsy, so the second test can never be false when it is evaluated. A dead condition that reads as a deliberate empty-string check. Harmless, but it survives at 100 % branch coverage only because a separate packaging test executes the launcher in its own process — i.e. the coverage floor did not catch it. **Closed 2026-08-29** as `explicit !== undefined && explicit !== ''`, matching the other two optional-string guards in the file. Honest caveat: the rewrite is behaviour-preserving and **no test can distinguish the two forms** — what changed is that both operands now carry weight, which the second did not. The in-process coverage was made real all the same: a new test in `test/index.test.ts` pins that an explicit `IG_ENV_FILE` is **exclusive** (the standard candidates are skipped), a branch previously "covered" only by the out-of-process packaging test. Mutating to `explicit !== ''` alone does not even compile — TS2345, because the `!== undefined` operand is what narrows the type | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-19 | A second copy of the token-shape vocabulary drifts from the first | `src/core/errors.ts` carries its own `TOKEN_SHAPE = /(?:EAA\|IGQ)[\w-]{20,}/g`, **strictly weaker** than the vocabulary in `src/core/redact.ts`: it misses `IGAA…`-prefixed tokens and the 64-hex-character `appsecret_proof`. Two dictionaries meant to recognise the same thing that do not, with the weaker one running at the moment it matters. The same file also carries the **second** false claim naming the non-existent `mcp/redact.ts` — and calls it "the authoritative redaction layer", which is worse than CC-PROC-17's wording because it tells the next maintainer the problem belongs to someone else. **Closed 2026-08-30.** The vocabulary in `errors.ts` is now the same three patterns as `core/redact.ts`, in the same order — order is load-bearing, because splicing the marker in is what introduces the word boundaries the `\b`-bounded 64-hex pattern needs. The copy stays a copy on purpose: this is a pure Layer-0 mapper, so it cannot depend on `createRedactor`, whose output varies with the process-global secret registry, and an `InstagramError` is read in places that never pass through a sink at all (a `catch` in a CLI, a test). What changed is that drift is no longer possible in silence — a test compares the two dictionaries through their **outputs** over an 18-entry corpus (`EAA…`, `IGQ…`, `IGAA…`, the length floor and one below it, lower/upper 64-hex, 63/65/32-hex, two shapes in one message, hashtags, permalinks, 17- and 18-digit ids, ISO timestamps), normalising the two different markers, so it holds however either side is spelled and bites in both directions. The false module name is gone and cannot come back: a second test scans every backticked `*.ts` path in the docstrings and fails if one does not resolve on disk — mechanical, not a denylist of the one bad name | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-20 | A token reaches the human through the **confirmation prompt** rather than through a tool result | `buildConfirmPrompt` in `src/mcp/write-mode.ts` masks only the **active profile's own** secrets — not other registered profiles, and not anything matching a token *shape* that belongs to no configured profile. That is strictly weaker than `logSafeError` two functions away in the same module, and weaker than the tool-result path since CC-PROC-17 was closed. It matters more than either: the elicitation prompt is the one message a **human reads and approves**, and it travels through `elicitInput`, a second server→client channel that `redactResult` cannot cover by construction. Constraint on any fix: the prompt renders relayed Instagram text inside an untrusted-content fence, and redaction must not break the fence or let a mask marker read as approved content — and must not make two different targets look identical to the approving human. **Closed 2026-08-30.** The finished message now runs the *same two passes, in the same order*, as `logSafeError` two functions away: the profile-scoped pass first (so the account the operator selected keeps its own `[redacted]` marker even when nothing ever registered its credentials), then a real `core/redact.ts` redactor, which adds every globally registered secret plus the token-**shape** backstop for values this process never owned — the mint→register window, and a token Graph echoed back into an error a tool relayed into its summary. The fence is untouched: redaction runs over the assembled message, after `sanitizeLine` has already stripped `\p{C}`, so it can only shorten spans, never introduce a delimiter. The "two targets must not look identical" constraint is met by arithmetic rather than by hope — a real Instagram object id is 17–18 digits and matches none of the three shapes; the only ids that mask are credential-shaped ones, and that over-redaction is pinned by its own test (a 64-hex target id renders as `[REDACTED]`, a 17-digit one renders verbatim). One existing test needed a one-character change and it is worth recording: the id length cap is 64, exactly an `appsecret_proof`'s length, so the old `'A'.repeat(500)` fixture came out of the cap as 64 hex characters and was masked — it now uses `'z'`, which is id-safe and not hex, so it measures the cut rather than the mask | post-M5 · design |
| CC-PROC-21 | A secret sits under a *key name* the redactor knows, inside a serialized text block | Key-name masking (`access_token`, `client_secret`, …) reaches `structuredContent` only. By the time `redactResult` sees the result, `json()` has already stringified the payload, so the text block is past the point where keys exist. Value-based and shape-based masking still cover both. Recorded rather than closed: closing it would mean re-parsing content blocks as JSON to re-serialise them, a worse trade than the residual. The docstrings state the asymmetry instead of glossing it | post-M5 · design |
| CC-PROC-22 | The **injected redactor itself** throws | **Closed 2026-08-31.** The whole record-building region of `emit` is guarded, deliberately **not** one guard per redactor call: the redactor is reached at three points — the fields object, the message string, and the reason text inside the serialization-failure path — and per-call guards would have missed the third, needed a sentinel or a duplicated write, and split one rationale across places that later drift. The owner question the row was held on ("which fields survive when redaction fails") is answered the only defensible way: **only what never touched the redactor** — `level`, `time`, a fixed `msg` stand-in, and the marker. The caller's fields and the caller's message are both dropped, since an interpolated message is exactly where a token would be, and `logErrorDetail` carries the thrown value's **class name only, never its message**: a redactor that throws may well have put the value it choked on into the text, and that value is the one just proven unredactable. The `write` is outside the guard on purpose, so this does not swallow the sink classification CC-PROC-24 depends on | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-23 | The log sink is slower than the server writes to it | `state.stream.write(...)`'s return value is discarded and there is no `drain` handling, so a blocked or slow stderr buffers log lines in memory without bound. A resource issue rather than a correctness one, and it is invisible under the stdio transport's usual conditions | post-M5 · design |
| CC-PROC-24 | `stderr` is closed under the server (client disconnect on the stdio transport) | **Closed 2026-08-31, and the closing found the worse half.** The synchronous throw the row describes is only one door: on a piped `stderr` — a supervised server, `\| tee`, any parent holding the read end — the failure arrives asynchronously as an `'error'` event, and with no listener attached Node terminates the process. Measured with a real child: parent destroys the read end, child keeps writing, **exit code 1**. Nothing in `src/` had ever attached an `'error'` listener, so the exact scenario this row was opened for was not merely unsurvivable, it was fatal. Both doors now classify through one strict whitelist (`EPIPE`, `ERR_STREAM_DESTROYED`, `ERR_STREAM_WRITE_AFTER_END`, `ERR_STREAM_ALREADY_FINISHED`) requiring a real `Error`, and latch. Anything outside it is rethrown from the handler and reaches Node exactly as if the module had never subscribed — a full disk still stops the process. The distinction the row demanded is kept: a sink that was **never** usable is not rescued, and the test pinning that a falsy stream fails loudly at the first write passes unchanged | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-25 | Every write travels in the **query string**, and the POST-body sink is dead code | All seven write functions in `src/api/comments.ts` and `src/api/publishing.ts` pass their payload as `params`, so a caption (Instagram permits 2200 characters), a `user_tags` JSON blob, a comment `message` and an `image_url` are percent-encoded onto the request line — alongside `access_token` and `appsecret_proof`. A long caption with emoji expands roughly 3–4× under percent-encoding, and a request line over an intermediary’s ceiling fails as an opaque 414/400 that names no parameter and is indistinguishable from a caption Graph disliked. Meanwhile the `body` half of `IgRequestOptions` is documented as the POST/DELETE payload path, is exercised only by unit tests, and is used by nothing — so the form-encoded path has never met real Graph behaviour. Two honest resolutions, and doing neither is the bad outcome: move the writes onto the body (and validate it live), or state that the field is reserved and stop implying otherwise. Owner decision — it changes what goes on the wire for every write | post-M5 · live |
| CC-PROC-26 | The **injected clock** throws | Found while closing CC-PROC-22, and separated from it on purpose: a broken clock costs the **timestamp**, not the record, so filing it under the redaction marker would misreport a healthy line as an unsafe one. Closed 2026-08-31 by writing `time: null` plus a marker of its own. The rejected alternative was falling back to `Date.now()`, which is **indistinguishable in the stream from a working injected clock**: an embedder with a frozen, offset or replayed clock would get plausible timestamps from a different clock domain — sorting wrongly, correlating with nothing, and leaving no trace of the substitution. `null` cannot be mistaken for a timestamp, and the module's rule holds: the sink does not invent a value it was not given. On a double failure the lost record outranks the lost timestamp — there is one `logError` slot | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-27 | A caller passes a field literally named `logError` | The health markers are written into the same flat record as caller fields, so before 2026-08-31 a caller field of that name could sit in the slot an operator greps to find broken lines — forging the health of a record, or masking a real failure. It became reachable the moment the clock marker started writing those keys on an otherwise **healthy** record (CC-PROC-26); on the CC-PROC-22 path the caller's fields are gone by construction. Closed in the same edit by adding `logError` and `logErrorDetail` to the reserved set that already shadowed `level`, `msg` and `time` — a colliding caller field is re-keyed, never dropped | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-28 | The `doctor` CLI's own telemetry (`log.debug` on entry, `log.info` on completion) sits outside every guard, while `core/log.ts` deliberately re-throws anything that is not a vanished sink (CC-PROC-24) | **Closed 2026-09-01.** Measured: an injected logger whose `debug` throws `ENOSPC: no space left on device` makes `runDoctor` propagate — no report at all, no exit code of its own; the startup handler then prints `instagram-mcp-ai failed to start: ...` and exits 1. The operator who ran the diagnostic **to find out why their token is not working** learns nothing about their token, because the disk filled up. That the real logger can throw is not speculation — `core/log.ts` swallows only a sink that has gone away and re-throws `ENOSPC` and non-stream failures on purpose. Both records now go through a local `telemetry(emit)` helper that contains the failure. The report stays the primary output and a broken sink adds nothing to it: telemetry is how the operator's *machine* narrates the run, and it must never outrank what the operator is standing there to read. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-29 | `doctor` decides on ANSI colour from the real `process.env` and `process.stdout.isTTY`, not from its injected `env` seam | Measured both directions: with `NO_COLOR=1` **injected** and the real environment clean, ANSI escapes are still emitted; with the injected environment empty and `NO_COLOR=1` in the **real** one, they are not. So the seam is not what decides. This is a seam inconsistency, not a wrong verdict — `DoctorDeps.env` is documented as scoped to the package summary, and colour is genuinely a property of the terminal the process is attached to rather than of the configuration being diagnosed. Left as is: widening the seam to cover colour would make `env` mean two different things, and narrowing `NO_COLOR` to the seam would break the convention that `NO_COLOR` is read from the process environment. **Owner decision** — say the word and it becomes a one-line change either way. | post-M5 · observed |
| CC-PROC-30 | A bearer token that is not ASCII cannot authenticate anybody | Node decodes an incoming header value as **latin1** — one character per wire byte — while `process.env` arrives already decoded as **UTF-8**. Comparing the two as UTF-8 buffers measures the same secret as 18 bytes on one side and 15 on the other, so `IG_HTTP_TOKEN=pärola-tökén-42` refuses the one client that sent exactly the right credential, answering it a constant `Unauthorized` that gives it nothing to debug. `bearerMatches` therefore encodes each side the way it arrived. What made this worth measuring rather than reasoning about: the test that shipped alongside the fix asserted the **opposite** pair of verdicts and failed, and the tempting repair — revert the comparison the failing test disagrees with — restores the broken code and turns the suite green. Four cases on one server settled it. `OutgoingMessage` holds the header block as a latin1 string and, when a body follows, writes header and body to the socket in a **single write using the body's encoding**, so a `utf8` string body re-encodes the header block as UTF-8. With a body — which every MCP request has — a client that puts the plain token in the header puts its 18 UTF-8 bytes on the wire (correct, must be accepted), while pre-encoding the token into the latin1 spelling of its own UTF-8 bytes yields 24 bytes, a form no client sends and which must be refused. With no body at all the header block goes out alone as latin1: 15 bytes. The test was rewritten around the real client and now pins 15 / 18 / 24 so a future reader cannot re-derive it backwards. The module's equivalent-mutant note was **corrected in the same edit**: it claimed replacing the whole function with `provided === expected` returns the same verdict for every input, which this case disproves — measured, that mutant now fails. Only the constant-time half is genuinely unobservable in process | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-31 | An unauthenticated `DELETE` reaches the MCP layer if the bearer check is scoped to `POST` | `startHttp` routes on nothing — every path and every verb is the MCP endpoint — and the SDK answers the odd verbs itself: stateless mode has no session to end, so it replies **200** to a `DELETE`. The bearer check is the only thing standing in front of that, and until 2026-09-02 the claim that it ran before the SDK on every shape was **prose in a test comment** rather than an assertion. The surface test that enumerates `OPTIONS`, `PUT`, `DELETE`, `GET`, a missing `Content-Type` and a body that is not JSON ran against a **token-less** listener, so a check narrowed to `req.method === 'POST'` survived the entire suite. Closed by sending each of those shapes twice against a listener that **does** carry a token: once with no credential, which must be `401` with the constant body, and once with the right one, which must reach the SDK and get its own non-401 answer — so a `401` arriving from anywhere other than the missing credential fails the second half too. Measured against the narrowed mutant: it now fails | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-32 | A client sends `Host` with no port | The DNS-rebinding allowlist carries the bare address as well as the `host:port` form, and dropping the bare one survived the whole suite — no test had ever sent the portless spelling. It is not hypothetical: a client omits the port whenever the bound one is the scheme's default (`IG_PORT=80`), which no unprivileged test can bind, and any client may send it on any port because Node forwards the header untouched. Without the bare form those requests are answered **403**, which reads to the operator as a rebinding attack that never happened. Pinned 2026-09-02 by a test that accepts a portless `Host: 127.0.0.1` and still refuses a portless `Host: attacker.example`, so the allowlist is proven to be an allowlist rather than a waved check | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-33 | A socket that sent half a request line pins HTTP shutdown for a minute | `httpServer.close()` stops accepting and drops idle keep-alive sockets, but it **waits** for any socket that is mid-request — including one that sent a partial request line and nothing more, which holds shutdown for the whole `headersTimeout` (60 s by default). `startHttp`'s `close()` therefore does three things in order: close every live MCP exchange back through the SDK, then `closeAllConnections()`, then await the `close()` callback. Mutation measured which of those actually provides the guarantee, and the answer corrected a comment that had been wrong since it was written. Dropping the in-flight loop alone **passes the whole suite** — `closeAllConnections()` yanks the socket out from under any exchange the loop would have closed politely. Dropping `closeAllConnections()` alone **hangs** the runner, which is how the case was found at all: some mutants do not fail, they stop the run, so the mutation harness needs `node --test --test-timeout=20000` or it dies at the tool ceiling with an empty report. Dropping both hangs too, so the property is measured; which member of the pair delivers it is not separable in process. The loop is kept — a graceful teardown and a severed socket look identical to `httpServer.close()` and nothing alike to a client reading the stream — and now carries an equivalent-mutant note saying exactly that instead of the stale claim that it is what prevents the hang | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-34 | A completed `sleep` leaves its abort listener attached, and nothing says so | `systemClock.sleep` registers `onAbort` with `{ once: true }`, which detaches the listener when `abort` **fires** — on the path where the timer simply elapses it never fires, so the closure stays attached for as long as the *signal* lives rather than as long as the sleep does. One signal reused across a retry loop therefore accumulates one dead closure per completed backoff. Nothing reports it: an `AbortSignal` is an `EventTarget`, not an `EventEmitter`, so it emits no `MaxListenersExceededWarning` at any count — measured on Node v22.23.2, fifteen resolved sleeps on one signal leave fifteen listeners and print nothing. The promise resolves either way, so every assertion that existed in `test/core/clock.test.ts` was satisfied by the leaking version; counting `getEventListeners(signal, 'abort')` is the only evidence available. Latent when found on 2026-09-02, and that is the point: no api-layer call sets `signal` today, so the only signal reaching here is `login`'s own short-lived one, and the leak becomes live the day cancellation is wired through `IgRequestOptions` to the retry loop in `core/http.ts` — where it would be attributed to the new feature rather than to this file. Fixed by calling `removeEventListener` in the timeout callback, pinned by a test that runs ten iterations (a fix detaching only the most recent listener passes a single-iteration test) and then proves the abort path still works on the same signal. Mutation: 12 mutants, 11 killed. `{ once: true }` -> `{ once: false }` is the one survivor and is genuinely equivalent — `abort` fires at most once per controller — while dropping the `addEventListener` call outright **hangs** the runner rather than failing it | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-35 | The one `debug_token` answer that needs the configured App ID is the one no test ever sent | `doctor`'s Path B report names the App Dashboard for the operator to open — `(App ID <id>)` — from `info.appId ?? appId`: the id introspection reported, falling back to the id configured for the profile. `app_id` is optional on the `debug_token` payload, and the shape where it is absent is a real one: a response that answered the validity question and nothing else. Every Path B test in `test/cli/doctor.test.ts` supplied an `app_id`, and the two that assert on the App ID line either used an introspected id that **disagreed** with configuration or a Path A (`ig-login`) profile that has no configured id at all — so the `??` fallback was never the operand under test. Deleting it left all 64 tests green. The cost of losing it is not a crash but a downgrade at exactly the wrong moment: the single line that tells an operator *which* dashboard to open becomes a line telling them to go find out, on the profile whose own configuration holds the answer. Found by mutation on 2026-09-02 (`appid-no-fallback`) and killed by a new test that routes a `debug_token` payload with no `app_id`. Module totals: 66 mutants, 61 killed. Three of the five survivors are genuine equivalents and now carry notes — `??` -> `\|\|` twice, unobservable because `core/config.ts`'s `clean()` has already mapped a blank field to `undefined` at the boundary, and `nearestExistingDir` dropping its `dirname`, unobservable from its one call site because `probeJournal` only reaches it after `statSync` answered `undefined` for that exact path. The fifth is a JS-level artifact TypeScript would not compile | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-36 | Three guards in `src/core/log.ts` that only an **untyped** caller can trip — and all three were unpinned | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-02; none is a defect, all three are now tests. (1) `isWatchableSink` tests `typeof stream === 'object'`, and a **callable** is the only value that distinguishes that spelling from the `stream !== null` one — a function is a perfectly good `WeakMap` key, so the doc's stated rationale did not actually cover it. Without the `typeof`, the module attaches a process-lifetime `'error'` handler to a value it has just judged not to be one of its streams, silently swallowing EPIPE-class errors raised on that object by anyone else. (2) `isLogLevel` tests `typeof level === 'string'` before `Object.hasOwn`, which **coerces its key**: drop the guard and the membership test runs caller code, so a level whose `toString` throws escapes `createLogger` before a logger exists to report the failure. Reachable in practice from the untyped `.mjs` entry points under `scripts/`. (3) `clock.now` passed **unbound** is indistinguishable for the closure-based fake clock the whole suite uses, and breaks any `Clock` that holds time on the instance. The damage is not a wrong timestamp: the unbound call throws on `this`, the clock guard catches it, and every line for that logger's life arrives `time: null` behind a broken-clock marker — a working clock reported broken | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-37 | A parallel agent killed mid-edit leaves a test file that `git status` calls clean | The session lost six background agents at once to a transient DNS failure, and one of them died between writing three tests and writing the helper they call: `test/tools/account.test.ts` gained 528 lines containing three call sites of `fakeAccountReq()` and no definition of it. `git status` said `M` — the same thing it says about a finished edit — so the damage was invisible in the one place a triage instinctively looks, and `git diff --stat` only confirmed that a large addition existed. `npm run check` does catch it, but at the **build** step, after `lint` and `format:check` have already run. The cheap detector is `npx tsc -p tsconfig.json --noEmit`, which named the three lines in well under a minute and, unlike a build, is safe to run at any time because it emits nothing and so cannot corrupt a shared `dist/` that other agents are still using. Hence the rule: after any agent death, run `--noEmit` **first**, before deleting the orphaned `.fixdist-*` outDirs and before the central check. The second half of the lesson is about the repair. The three orphaned tests were not junk to be deleted — they were byte-for-byte contract pins for three published tool schemas, the only tests that would ever observe those schemas as shipped, and deleting them to make the compiler quiet would have silently removed the coverage while looking like a fix. The helper was written instead, and deliberately made to **throw**: those tests only call `listTools`, so the responder is never reached, and a future test that does drive a handler should fail loudly rather than quietly pin a schema against `undefined`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-38 | An empty `host` argument silently redirected the request instead of being refused | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-07. `const host = opts.host ?? auth.defaultHost` and `opts.host \|\| auth.defaultHost` are indistinguishable to the whole suite, because the only value they disagree about is `''` and no test had ever passed one. They are not interchangeable: `??` lets the empty host through to `assertAllowedHost`, which refuses it, while `\|\|` swaps in the default host and sends the request to a host **the caller never named**. A `host` that arrives empty is a config or call-site bug, and the failure mode of quietly rewriting it is the same class of confusion the SSRF gate exists to prevent — a request is issued somewhere nobody asked for, and the mistake is invisible because it succeeded. Pinned by a test that passes `host: ''` and asserts `kind=validation` with **zero** fetch calls, alongside the existing disallowed-host and loopback cases | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-39 | Two shapes of test blindness a green suite cannot report: the double that hands the mutant its own answer, and the assertion that never looks at the number | Both found in the `core/http.ts` sweep of 2026-09-07 and both worth generalising. (1) **The fixture supplies the constant.** `recordingClock().now` returned `0`, so replacing `clock.now()` with the literal `0` was unkillable *by construction* — not because the arithmetic was untested but because the test double had already agreed with the bug. Every HTTP-date `Retry-After` fixture was anchored at the epoch for readability, and readability was exactly what hid it. The fix is a clock anchored in 2026: read correctly the wait is 30 s, read as `0` it is 56 years, which the 60 s cap flattens to a full minute. Rule of thumb: **a stub whose value equals the mutant's replacement can never kill it**, so a constant chosen for legibility (`0`, `''`, `1`) deserves a second fixture that is deliberately none of those. (2) **The assertion never sees the quantity.** The timeout test drove a `fetch` that never answers, which proves a deadline exists but says nothing about its value — `20 ms`, `200 ms` and `20 s` all end identically. Multiplying `settings.timeoutMs` by ten survived. Now a fetch that answers at 120 ms against a 20 ms deadline separates them: honest client aborts, inflated one returns 200 | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-40 | A per-module mutation run in this repo systematically **over-reports** survivors, because the tests that kill them belong to no module | Five consecutive backlogs (`tools/media`, `tools/publishing`, `api/discovery`, `api/comments`, `core/config-write`) were each handed over with a list of "survivors" that a scoped run — `node --test dist/test/<area>/**` — had produced, and in every one of them the great majority were already dead. The comments backlog was the extreme case: **41 of 41** re-checked mutants died the moment the FULL suite ran, and one of the checkpoint's own lines had already said why (`killed by out-of-scope test/api/path-injection.test.ts`). The cause is structural, not accidental. This suite deliberately carries **cross-cutting contract files** that assert one property across all 28 tools at once — `test/tools/id-schemas.test.ts` (every id-shaped input rejects a traversal payload), `test/mcp/tool-metadata-contract.test.ts` (titles, descriptions and annotations on every spec), `test/api/path-injection.test.ts` (no user value reaches a Graph path segment unencoded), `test/env-catalog.test.ts` (every documented env var is read somewhere). None of them lives under the directory of the module it protects, so no per-module scope includes them — which is exactly the property that makes them valuable and exactly the property that makes a scoped run lie. The protocol that follows: **scope the search, never the verdict.** A scoped run is a cheap filter for finding candidates; every candidate it flags is then re-run against the whole suite before anyone writes a test for it. Skipping that step costs far more than it saves — it invites a test written to kill a mutant that four other files already kill, and a redundant test is worse than no test, because it looks like coverage. | closed · process |
| CC-PROC-41 | A version-pin guard that says `startsWith` is one keystroke from a containment test — and the suite could not tell the two apart | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `assertPinnedGraphPath` (`src/core/host.ts`). The prefix check is `` `${parsed.pathname}/`.startsWith(`/v25.0/`) ``, and relaxing it to `.includes(...)` survived all 1636 tests. The three inputs the existing test used — `/../me`, `/X/../../me` and `me` — are killed by containment too, because none of them leaves a second copy of the version anywhere in the path. The escaping shape is a `path` that forgot its leading slash **and** carries the version again further along: `extra/v25.0/x` assembles to `https://graph.instagram.com/v25.0extra/v25.0/x`, whose pathname CONTAINS `/v25.0/` while its first segment is the unrelated `v25.0extra`. The second guard does not catch it either — there is no dot segment and no character the parser rewrites, so `origin + pathname` compares equal to the base and the URL is emitted. A containment spelling therefore ships a request off the version pin, which is the one thing this function exists to prevent. The generalisable half: **a positional predicate needs a fixture where the sought substring also occurs somewhere else.** Every test for a `startsWith`/`endsWith` guard whose inputs contain the needle exactly once is really only testing containment. Closed by adding `extra/v25.0/x` to `buildUrl refuses a path that escapes the pinned version prefix`. Module totals for the sweep: 47 mutants over `core/host.ts` and `core/errors.ts`, 46 killed, this the sole gap; the one other survivor (dropping `'error' in body` from `parseErrorEnvelope`) was already documented as equivalent in the source | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-42 | `??` versus `\|\|` is not a style question, and three sweeps now disagree about which spelling is the bug | The same one-token mutation has been adjudicated three ways in this repo, and the rule that separates them is worth stating once. It is a **gap** when the falsy value the two operators disagree about is a value a caller can legitimately choose: `opts.host \|\| auth.defaultHost` silently redirects an empty host to the default instead of refusing it (CC-PROC-38), and `deps.timeoutMs \|\| CAPTURE_TIMEOUT_MS` in `src/cli/login.ts` turns an explicit `timeoutMs: 0` into a five-minute wait — a regression that manifests only as a hang, which is why the test that pins it carries an `isPending()` fuse rather than a bare `assert.rejects` (`npm test` passes no `--test-timeout`, so an unfused version would hang CI instead of failing it). It is an **equivalent** when the type admits no falsy value other than nullish — `deps.env ?? process.env` is `NodeJS.ProcessEnv \| undefined`, so every non-nullish value is a truthy object — or when a boundary upstream has already normalised the falsy case away, which is what `clean()` in `src/core/config.ts` does for every blank config field before `doctor` ever reads one. And it is a **deliberate non-pin** when the mutant's behaviour is the better one: `info.appId \|\| appId` renders a real dashboard id where `??` would render `(App ID )`, so pinning `??` would freeze the worse output. `??` still stays in all three cases, because it states the intent — *the fallback is for a MISSING value* — but the test decision follows the value set, not the operator | closed · process |
| CC-PROC-43 | A test that asks an override for the value the fallback already returns proves the override is *reachable*, not that it is *read* | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 across `src/core/config.ts`. `IG_PROFILE_<NAME>_AUTH_MODE` is a documented alias for `_AUTH_PATH`, and two tests covered it — `a named profile accepts IG_PROFILE_<NAME>_AUTH_MODE as well as _AUTH_PATH` and `a profile named "auth" is not swallowed by the AUTH_MODE alias`. Both set the alias to `fb-login` on a profile that also carried `_APP_ID` and `_APP_SECRET`. But an app id plus an app secret is exactly what `resolveAuthPath` infers `fb-login` from when no explicit value is present, so emptying `SUFFIX_ALIASES` outright — or dropping the `SUFFIX_ALIASES[spelling] ?? spelling` lookup — left both tests green: the alias was thrown away, the explicit value was lost, and inference happened to reproduce the asserted answer. The alias is not decoration; it is the spelling the env catalogue (architecture §12) and `.env.example` both document, so a build that silently ignored it would take an operator's stated `ig-login` and run Path B against it. The generalisable half: **a fixture for an override must request the one value the fallback would not have chosen.** Any test where the expected result is also the default's result is a test of the default. Closed by flipping both fixtures to ask for `ig-login` while leaving the app credentials in place, so inference and the alias now disagree and only the alias can produce the assertion. Two more gaps in the same sweep were CC-PROC-41's positional-predicate family on a second module — `key.startsWith(NAMED_PREFIX)` relaxed to `.includes(...)` and `rest.endsWith(\`_${s}\`)` relaxed to `.includes(...)` both survived, because every fixture spelled the needle exactly once and at the end.
