The escaping shapes are the two ways an operator parks a variable instead of deleting it: `OLD_IG_PROFILE_BRAND_ACCESS_TOKEN` (renamed) slices the profile name from a fixed offset that no longer lines up, yielding `ile_brand`; `IG_PROFILE_BRAND_ACCESS_TOKEN_OLD` (suffixed) yields `brand_acc` holding the *superseded* token, and the same slip on `_APP_ID_OLD` produces a profile with no token of its own, so `loadProfiles` throws and the server refuses to start over a leftover key. Module totals: 32 mutants, 22 killed on the first pass, 6 real gaps closed by 3 new tests and 3 strengthened ones, 4 survivors all pre-documented as equivalent in the source (the `READABLE_SUFFIXES` length sort, the `>` in the name-length guard, the `name === ''` half of its partner test, and `active ?? DEFAULT_PROFILE_NAME` per CC-PROC-42) | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-15 | A blank `IG_ACCESS_TOKEN` and a missing one are the same failure, and only one of them produced the first-run message | `loadProfiles` opens with `if (clean(defaultRaw.ACCESS_TOKEN) === undefined) throw 'No default profile configured; set IG_ACCESS_TOKEN'`, and `buildProfile` re-checks the same field a few lines later with `Profile 'default' has no access token`. The second is a fallback for named profiles and reads wrong for the default one — it names a profile the operator never created — which is why `validation: the nothing-configured message is the first-run one` exists. That test passes a wholly absent token, so dropping `clean(...)` from the up-front guard survived it: `undefined === undefined` still fires. The whitespace-only spelling is what separates them, and its test asserted only `kind: 'validation'`, which both messages satisfy. An `IG_ACCESS_TOKEN=` line with nothing after it is the single most likely shape of a first-run `.env`, so the case that got the worse message was the common one. **Closed 2026-09-08** by making `validation: a whitespace-only default token is rejected` assert the message, not just the kind. No source change — the guard was already correct | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-25 | Masking a credential out of a Graph error threw away the two fields the operator needs to act on it | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `redactExchangeSecrets` (`src/core/refresh.ts`). When a Graph error message echoes a credential the redactor cannot edit the `InstagramError` in place, so it constructs a new one and copies every field across by hand. Two of those copies were unpinned: `subcode: err.subcode` and `fbtraceId: err.fbtraceId` could each be replaced with `undefined` and the whole suite stayed green, while their siblings on the same rebuild (`kind`, `status`, `code`, `cause`) were all pinned. The loss is silent and lands on the worst day. The message still reads correctly and the kind is still right, but the operator loses the `error_subcode` that separates a spam restriction from a quota from an expired container (operations §3) and the `fbtrace_id` Meta support asks for first — on the one failure path where a credential was on the wire, which is to say the path they are most likely to be escalating. The trace id is the sharper case: it usually arrives in a response *header*, so unlike the body fields there is no `cause` copy to recover it from once the rebuild drops it. **Closed 2026-09-08** with `masking a Graph error preserves the subcode and trace id it was carrying` in `test/core/refresh.test.ts` — a 400 whose `error.message` echoes the app secret (forcing the rebuild), carrying `error_subcode: 33` and its trace id in the header. No source change; the code was already right. Module totals: 115 mutants over `core/auth.ts` and `core/refresh.ts`, 111 killed on the first pass, these 2 the only real gaps, 4 survivors all pre-documented as equivalent. Everything security-bearing died — host choice both directions, the `host === FB_HOST` proof gate, the HMAC algorithm and digest encoding, signing the secret instead of the token, the argument order into `createHmac`, and all three `SECRET_PARAM_KEYS` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-44 | A fake that records what it was asked is not coverage until something asserts the recording | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/mcp/write-mode.ts`. `FakeConfirmer` in `test/mcp/write-mode.test.ts` collected every prompt into `prompts`, and no test read it. The array made the file look as though the confirmation text was covered; what was actually covered was that *a* prompt existed. Replacing the argument to `confirmer.ask` with an unrelated string survived the suite — which is to say the gate could have asked the operator to approve a different account, or a different action, and every test still passed. On a consent gate the prompt is not presentation, it is the thing being consented to. The same sweep found the two redaction passes in `buildConfirmPrompt` and `logSafeError` swappable without a failure: the module's own comment says "Ordering matters" (the profile pass leaves `[redacted]`, `core/redact.ts` leaves `[REDACTED]`), but the existing fixtures used a token too short to trip the shape backstop, so only one pass ever ran and the order was unobservable. **Closed 2026-09-08** — the prompt is now compared against `buildConfirmPrompt(intent, ctx)` with the `Action:` / `Account:` lines pinned separately, the redaction fixtures use an `EAA…`-shaped token that both passes match, and `refusedResult`'s dropped `details` is asserted. The generalisable half: **grep every collection a fake fills for a reader.** One with no reader is a claim of coverage that the coverage tool will happily confirm, because the line that appends to it did execute | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-45 | The missing guard did not fail a test — it downed the process, which is not the same thing | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/mcp/transport.ts`. The HTTP error path is `if (!res.headersSent) refuse(res, 500, …)`, inside the `catch` around `transport.handleRequest`. Remove the guard and `writeHead` throws `ERR_HTTP_HEADERS_SENT` *from within the catch block*, so it escapes as an unhandled rejection — and on Node 22 an unhandled rejection terminates the process by default. The shape is reachable whenever the SDK transport has already begun streaming a response and then fails, which is the normal end of a long SSE session. The mutant is therefore not a cosmetic one: the difference between guarded and unguarded is a server that logs a failed request and a server that exits. It matters for the sweep protocol as much as for the code. A regression of this kind does not surface as `not ok`; it surfaces as a runner that dies or hangs, so a driver reading only `# fail` scores it SURVIVED. This is the second reason the standing rule reads both `# fail` **and** `# cancelled`. **Closed 2026-09-08** with a test that stubs `StreamableHTTPServerTransport.prototype.handleRequest` to answer and then throw, and asserts no unhandled rejection is raised. Same sweep: the successful stdio ready log was unasserted (only the failing `connect` was), and `the ready log names the address the socket got` accepted `'localhost'`, which is what the assertion was written to exclude. Module totals for the `src/mcp` layer: 152 mutants, `registry.ts` 50/50, `write-mode.ts` 54 killed + 1 equivalent, `transport.ts` 45 killed + 1 equivalent; 8 real gaps closed by 6 new tests and 2 strengthened ones, no production code changed | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-46 | A guard that asks "was it set?" must not be relaxed to "is it truthy?" — the empty answer is still an answer | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/core/log.ts`, and the same day in `src/api/publishing.ts` (CC-PUB-27), which is what makes it a rule rather than an incident. `emit` writes the broken-clock marker under `if (clockFailure !== undefined)`; relaxing it to `if (clockFailure)` survived all 1647 tests. The value is `failureName(error)`, i.e. `printableText(error.name)` — and `name` is an ordinary writable property that an embedder's error class is free to leave empty. So the mutant loses the announcement for precisely the errors that are hardest to identify: the record arrives with `time: null` and nothing at all saying why, which an operator reads as the logger emitting a null timestamp of its own accord — the papering-over the guard exists to prevent. The suite could not see it because every clock fixture threw an `Error` or a `TypeError`, whose `name` is never empty. **Closed 2026-09-08** with `a clock failure is announced even when the thrown value has no name to report`, whose clock throws an `Error` with `name` set to `''` and which asserts the marker is present with `logErrorDetail: ''`. The generalisable half: **wherever presence and truth can differ — `''`, `0`, `false` — an `!== undefined` guard is load-bearing, and the fixture that pins it has to carry the falsy-but-present value.** A test that supplies only non-empty details proves the guard is reachable, not that it is the right guard (the CC-PROC-43 shape, one level down). Module totals for `src/core/log.ts`: 87 mutants, 86 killed on the first pass, 1 real gap, no production defect; the three `Equivalent-mutant note:` halves already argued in the source were left unmutated on the strength of those arguments | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-23 | An empty `after` cursor from Graph would be reported as the end of the hashtag's media | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/api/discovery.ts`. `getHashtagMedia` publishes the continuation cursor on `nextAfter !== undefined`, and withholding `after` is that function's **only** end-of-stream signal — a caller reads its absence as "there is no next page", which is exactly the signal CC-DATA-4's truncation rule deliberately borrows. Relaxed to plain truthiness the guard swallows a present-but-empty cursor and manufactures a last page Graph never announced, and no fixture carried a falsy-but-present cursor: the existing cases covered a real cursor, a caller-supplied cursor, an absent `paging` key and a truncated page. The api layer's contract is pass-through (CC-DATA-2, CC-DATA-6) — report the field as Meta delivered it — and the judgement about whether a cursor can be *spent* belongs one layer up, where `src/tools/discovery.ts` already runs `isUsableHandle(page.after)` before publishing it (CC-DATA-11, CC-DATA-20). The two layers draw different lines on purpose; collapsing the api layer's line into the tool layer's would leave the anomaly invisible to both. Closed by one test pinning `after: ''` through to the result. Third instance of CC-PROC-46 in two days | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-47 | A guard whose entire product is a sentence needs the sentence asserted, not the throw | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/mcp/result.ts`. `json()` refuses a payload `JSON.stringify` declines to render — a function, a symbol, a forced `undefined` — by throwing a `TypeError`, and the suite asserted only the constructor, so replacing the whole message with `'json(): bad payload'` survived. The distinction matters more here than for an ordinary error, because the neighbouring cases in the same test never reach this line: a cyclic object and a BigInt throw from inside `JSON.stringify` carrying V8's own explanation, so the only values that exercise this guard are precisely the ones that arrive with no explanation at all. Nothing catches the `TypeError` to inspect it either — `mcp/registry.ts` renders it into an `isError` text block the operator and the model read — so the sentence **is** the diagnostic, and "renders to nothing" is what separates "JSON refused to serialize this" from "JSON silently declined to write it". The rule generalises: `assert.throws(fn, TypeError)` proves the branch is reachable, not that it says anything; wherever a branch's only output is text a human reads, assert the text. Closed by one message matcher on the existing test. Same family as CC-PROC-43 | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-48 | The type guard on `registerSecret` was copied into `createRedactor`, and only one copy was tested | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/core/redact.ts` (frozen module — no production change). `createRedactor` filters `extraSecrets` with `typeof s === 'string'` and a length floor; dropping the `typeof` half survived every existing test. Separability was proven rather than argued: a `null` or `undefined` element makes the constructor **throw a TypeError**, and it is constructed inside `doctor` — precisely when the operator is already staring at suspect credentials; an 8-element array becomes the literal needle `a,b,c,d,e,f,g,h`; and `{ length: 9 }` becomes `[object Object]`, so the redactor starts masking unrelated text while masking no secret at all. (`42` and `{}` are inseparable — the length floor discards them on its own.) `registerSecret` has a tested guard and its copy did not, which is the shape of CC-PROC-19 again: two places that must agree, one of them unwatched. Closed by one test in `test/core/redact.test.ts` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-26 | `expiryLabel` truncating a fractional second would have gone unnoticed | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/core/time.ts`. Wrapping `expiresAtSec` in `Math.trunc` or in `Math.round` both survived. The integer contract was pinned — `+1` and `Math.abs` die at once — but nothing pinned that a **fractional** second reaches `isoFromEpochSeconds` intact, while that helper explicitly documents and tests that fractional seconds survive as milliseconds. Two independent mutants sitting on one uncovered strip is what makes this a gap rather than a curiosity: a rounding step inserted here would silently disagree with the helper it delegates to, and token-expiry arithmetic is where an answer that is off by a second is read as authoritative. Closed by one assertion — `expiryLabel(1_760_184_000.25)` renders `'2025-10-11T12:00:00.250Z'` — on the existing test, now named for what it proves | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-INS-9 | The insights readers pin no Graph host, and only the account module's tests said so | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/api/insights.ts`. Adding `host: 'graph.facebook.com'` to any of the four insights requests survived the whole suite. The pin is not cosmetic: `src/core/http.ts` resolves the host as `opts.host ?? auth.defaultHost`, so an explicit host **overrides the active auth path**, and all four tools are path-neutral (`ToolSpec.paths` undefined), so nothing upstream would refuse the call first — a single pin would break `ig-login` (graph.instagram.com) outright for every insights read. The invariant was already nailed down next door: the same mutation dies on `getAccount`, and `listLinkedAccounts`' deliberate `graph.facebook.com` pin is covered in both directions. So this was pure asymmetry between two neighbouring api modules, not a design gap — the property was believed to be suite-wide because one module's tests happened to state it. Closed by one test asserting `host === undefined` on all four readers | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-49 | A `z.enum` gate is only as strong as its negative case, and half the insights inputs had none | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-08 in `src/tools/insights.ts`. Four enum-shaped inputs — `period`, `metric_type`, `timeframe` and demographics' `metrics` — survived being downgraded to a bare `z.string()`, while `breakdown` and the account/media `metrics` died immediately. The difference was entirely in the tests: the survivors had positive cases only, the others had a rejected value. An enum that accepts anything is worse than no enum, because the tool description still advertises the closed set while the invalid value is spent on a Graph call that fails upstream with a message about a metric name the operator never typed. The same asymmetry hid a third case: `notes: z.array(z.string())` accepted `z.array(z.number())`, because the output-schema test used only `notes: []` (which passes both) and a malformed object (which fails both), never a real string. Rule: a schema constraint is untested until one test supplies a value it must **reject**, and an array element type is untested until one test supplies an element of the right type. Closed by two negative-case tests and one strengthened positive | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-16 | Only the first env file that exists was proven to load, so a project `.env` could have been dropped silently | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/index.ts`. `loadEnvFiles` walks the candidate list (`<configHome>/instagram-mcp-ai/.env`, then `<cwd>/.env`) and loads **every** file that exists, each with `override: false`; inserting a `break` after the first hit survived the whole suite. The existing precedence case — "the config home outranks the project `.env`" — cannot see it, because there both files carry the same keys and the second one contributes nothing either way. The lost behaviour is the useful half of the rule: `login`/`refresh` own the credential file in the config home, and the checked-out project `.env` is where a developer keeps the knobs (`IG_LOG_LEVEL`, `IG_WRITE_MODE`, `IG_MAX_ITEMS`), so first-hit-wins would leave those knobs inert with no message anywhere. Separating the two needs a key exactly ONE file sets. Closed by a test that writes a credential to the config home and `IG_LOG_LEVEL=debug` to the project file, then asserts both took effect in a single `doctor` run | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-50 | A level filter is untested until a test asserts an ABSENCE at the strict setting | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/index.ts`. `createLogger({ level: settings.logLevel })` is the only place `IG_LOG_LEVEL` becomes a real filter, and hardcoding `level: 'debug'` survived every test: each of them either asks for `debug` itself or reads records that survive any level, and a test that only looks for records it expects to FIND is blind to a filter that has stopped filtering. The hidden failure is not cosmetic — `graph request` names the host and path of every call the server makes, so an entry that ignores the configured level narrates the operator's whole Graph traffic into stderr on a server they asked to keep quiet, and buries the warnings they did ask for in it. The general rule: for anything whose product is *suppression* — a log level, a redactor, a write gate's refusal, a truncation cap — the load-bearing assertion is that the suppressed thing is missing, plus a control run at the permissive setting proving it is a filter doing its job and not a path that simply produces nothing. Closed by one two-run test (`warn` emits no `debug` record, `debug` emits one) | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-51 | A message constant asserted against itself proves the constant exists, not that anything uses it | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/ids.ts`. `GRAPH_ID_MESSAGE` is the rejection text every object-id argument shares, and the only assertion on it was `text.includes(GRAPH_ID_MESSAGE)` with the constant imported from the module under test — so replacing the whole sentence with anything at all kept the test green, because both sides of the comparison moved together. This is CC-PROC-43's family seen from the other end: there the text was never asserted, here it is asserted only against itself. The text is load-bearing for the same reason CC-PROC-43 gives — `mcp/registry.ts` renders zod issue *messages* (never the offending value) into the tool error, so this sentence is the model's entire account of why its id was refused and what to send instead; a message that degraded to `'invalid'` would cost a turn per rejection and, worse, would stop telling the model that `/`, `?`, `#`, `&` and `.` are the characters at issue. Rule: when a test imports the constant it is checking, it is comparing a value to itself — assert at least one literal fragment a human wrote. Closed by pinning literal fragments of the sentence | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-COM-9 | An empty-but-present name, caption or comment body escaped its untrusted-content fence | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/account.ts` and `src/tools/comments.ts`. Seven guards of the form `x !== undefined` relaxed to plain truthiness survived — `fenceOptional`'s four profile fields, `commentToRecord`'s and `commentDetailToRecord`'s `text`/`username`, and `taggedMediaToRecord`'s `caption`/`username` — because no fixture carried a falsy-but-PRESENT value. The two modules fail differently, which is what makes the class worth a row of its own. In `comments` the record starts as `{ ...c }`, so the field is already there and the guard only decides whether to **overwrite it with the fenced form**: an empty string therefore reaches the model raw, outside the fence — harmless for `''` itself, but the record's stated guarantee is "every account-controlled string here is fenced", and a guard whose real condition is *content* rather than *presence* stops holding that line the moment the field's type widens. In `account` the same relaxation deletes the key outright, collapsing present-but-empty into absent — the exact confusion CC-DATA-23 draws for cursors, and here it makes "this account has no bio" indistinguishable from "Meta withheld the bio", which is a permissions question. Closed by one fixture per module carrying `''` in each field. Fourth and fifth instance of CC-PROC-46 | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-52 | A test that reads the TypeScript SOURCE is invisible to a compiled-JS mutation harness, and reports false survivors | Noted 2026-09-09 while sweeping `src/index.ts`. Every sweep in this programme mutates the COMPILED output in a private out dir (`npx tsc --outDir .fixdist-<TAG>`, patch `.fixdist-<TAG>/src/**/*.js`, restore from a pristine `.keep.js`) — ~400x faster than editing TypeScript and rebuilding per mutant, and the reason a 150-mutant sweep is a morning rather than a week. `test/env-catalog.test.ts` is the one test that does not play by those rules: it reads `src/**/*.ts` from the repo root with `readFileSync` and audits the `IG_*` tokens it finds against `.env.example`, bidirectionally. Run from a private out dir it therefore audits the **unmutated** source, so a mutant that renames an env token in the compiled JS reads as SURVIVED when the real suite would have killed it on the spot — a false gap, which costs more than a missed one because it invites a test that pins nothing. Two rules follow. Judging: any survivor that touches an env-var name, a file path under `src/`, or anything else read off disk rather than executed must be re-judged against the real suite before it is called a gap. Designing: mutate an env name to a **different existing** `IG_*` name (`IG_ENV_FILE` -> `IG_HTTP_TOKEN`) rather than to an invented one, so the catalogue audit stays satisfied either way and the mutant is decided by behaviour instead of by an artefact of the harness. Same family as CC-PROC-40 — scope the search, never the verdict | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-COM-10 | Three comment writes never read Graph's answer, so a refused write would have been reported as a completed one | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/api/comments.ts`. `deleteComment`, `setCommentHidden` and `setCommentsEnabled` are typed to return Graph's acknowledgement (`CommentWriteResult = { success?: boolean }`), and every existing assertion about them inspected the **request** they build — method, path, params. That left the response path wholly unpinned: replacing the body with a locally synthesized `{ success: true }` after the `await` satisfied the declared type, the request assertions and the compiler alike, and survived the suite. Meta answers a refusal on these endpoints with `success: false` plus an `error_user_msg` (the caller does not own the object, the comment is already deleted, the media does not accept the toggle), and nothing downstream re-reads it — no tool handler inspects `success`, so the api layer's return value **is** what the model sees. A synthesized ack therefore turns "Meta declined to hide this comment" into "hidden", and a model that trusts the ack stops there. The absent-key case matters for the same reason from the other side: an acknowledgement with no `success` at all is Meta disclosing nothing (CC-DATA-2 on the write side) and must not be back-filled into a positive one. Closed by one test that `deepEqual`s the whole ack for all three writers — extra Graph keys included, so a reshaped acknowledgement fails too — plus a silent-ack case asserting `'success' in result === false`. **Corrected 2026-09-23:** the premise "no tool handler inspects `success`" was itself the defect — the handlers discarded the ack and echoed their argument, so a refusal never reached the model at all (CC-COM-13) | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-53 | A fixture that is a fixed point of every transformation cannot catch any of them | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/api/comments.ts`. Five separate text-mangling mutants on the two comment writers — `message.trim()`, `.normalize('NFKC')`, `.slice(0, 100)` and two spelling variants — all survived, and not one of them needed a missing assertion to do it: `replyToComment` and `createComment` were asserted with `'hello'` and `'nice post'`, and a short plain-ASCII word is a **fixed point** of trimming, of Unicode normalization and of any truncation longer than itself. The assertions were present, correct, and incapable of failing. What the api layer owes here is transport fidelity — the message is model- or operator-authored and gets published verbatim under the operator's name — and the tool layer only checks `z.string().min(1)`, so a long multi-line reply with real typography is ordinary input, not an edge case. A quiet edit costs the operator a public post they did not write: an answer cut mid-sentence, a deliberate blank line before a signature stripped, a ligature or combining accent rewritten inside a customer's own name. Rule: for any value a layer promises to pass through untouched, pick a fixture that is a fixed point of **nothing** — surrounding whitespace, a character NFKC rewrites, and length past any plausible cap — and assert that property of the fixture in the test itself, so the pin cannot be softened later without a failure. Same family as CC-PROC-43 and CC-PROC-41: the fixture, not the assertion, is what was missing. A second instance landed the same day one layer up: in `src/tools/discovery.ts`, inserting `.toLowerCase()` on the handle before the Graph call survived every test, because every fixture in the file was already lowercase — while `INSTAGRAM_USERNAME_PATTERN` (`/^[A-Za-z0-9._]{1,30}$/`) accepts `A-Z`, so a mixed-case handle is ordinary input the tool advertises. Closed by a fixture of `NASA_Gov.1` asserted verbatim in the field expression. Module totals for the sweep: 99 mutants over `src/api/comments.ts`, 86 killed on the first pass, 8 real gaps closed by 2 new tests, 5 survivors all equivalent — the four normalizer swaps already argued in the source plus a fifth site the note had missed (handing `normalizeCommentDetail` to `fetchPagedEdge` as `listComments`' projection), which is now recorded there too | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-24 | A cursor of the wrong TYPE is the mirror of an empty one, and only the empty case was covered | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/discovery.ts`. `get_hashtag_media` publishes its continuation cursor only through `isUsableHandle(page.after)` — `typeof value === 'string' && value !== ''` — and reducing that guard to a plain `if (page.after)` survived the whole suite. The existing cases covered `''` and `null`, which are falsy under both spellings, so nothing separated them. The value that does is a cursor that is truthy but not a string: `paging.cursors.after: 42`. It is reachable because the api layer is a pass-through by contract (CC-DATA-2, CC-DATA-6) and `req` **casts** the Graph body rather than validating it, so a non-string `after` arrives here intact. Publishing it puts a number into a field the output schema types as `string`, and `mcp/registry.ts` validates outputs — so the failure is not one malformed field, it is the entire page of media the operator asked for, refused at the boundary with a schema error rather than returned without a cursor. This is CC-PROC-46 seen from the other side: that rule says a presence guard needs a falsy-but-**present** fixture, this one says a type guard needs a truthy-but-**wrong-type** fixture. A guard that narrows `unknown` is doing two jobs at once, and any single fixture can only exercise one of them. Closed by extending `get_hashtag_media withholds a cursor that cannot be spent again` with the numeric cursor, asserting `after` is still withheld Second instance the same day, in `src/api/media.ts`: `isUsableCursor` — `typeof after === 'string' && after !== ''` — could be replaced wholesale by `Boolean(after)` because the only cursor fixtures were `null` and `''`, both falsy under either spelling. The separating value is `paging.cursors.after: 12345`, which the walk would have put on the wire as `&after=12345` and then published into a `z.string().optional()` output field, so the SDK refuses the entire tool call; the closing test pins both halves — `calls.length === 1` for the send and `'after' in res === false` for the publish. Module totals for that sweep: 187 mutants over `src/api/media.ts` and `src/api/media-spec.ts`, 185 killed on the first pass, 1 real gap and 1 proven equivalent, with `media-spec.ts` killing all 79 of its own mutants outright. No production defect. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-54 | A message pinned by fragments is not pinned — everything between the fragments is free | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/discovery.ts`. The advisory-budget note was covered by three `includes` assertions, and two mutants that deleted the text **between** them — the leading `In-process only — ` and the `(v1). ` version marker — both survived, because every fragment still matched. The same file had the shape in its stronger form: `search_hashtag`'s refusal for a hashtag that normalizes to nothing could have its whole sentence replaced by `'bad hashtag'` while the test's `err.kind === 'validation'` and `messages.size === 1` both still held. In each case the text is the entire product of the code path — the budget note exists to tell the model what the counter is *not* (per-process, not the account-wide quota Meta enforces), and the refusal is the model's only instruction on how to retry — so a message that degrades into a shorter, truer-sounding one is a silent loss of exactly the caveat that stops a wrong action. Rule: an `includes` assertion pins its needle and nothing else; where the message IS the deliverable, assert the whole string (or a set equality over the messages collected). Third angle on one seam: CC-PROC-47 says assert the sentence rather than the throw, CC-PROC-51 says do not assert a constant against itself, and this one says a handful of fragments is not the sentence. Module totals for the sweep: 139 mutants over `src/tools/discovery.ts`, 129 killed on the first pass, 10 survivors → 7 real gaps closed by 1 new and 5 extended tests, 3 equivalents proven and noted in the source. Two of the gaps were plain CC-PROC-49 instances — the output `ids: z.array(z.string())` widened to `z.array(z.any())` and the media item's `caption: z.string()` widened to `z.any()`, both invisible until a test supplied an element the schema must reject. No production defect | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-25 | An opaque token is a fixed point of case folding when every fixture is written in one case | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/media.ts`. Three mutants — `.toUpperCase()` inserted on the inbound `paging.after`, on the outbound `args.after` and on the outbound `args.mediaId` — survived the **full** suite, because every id and cursor fixture in the file (`M1`, `A1`, `CURSOR-A1`) is already upper-case and therefore a fixed point of the transformation the mutant applies. Cursors and object ids are opaque: the only contract the server has is to hand back the exact bytes Graph issued and to send back the exact bytes the model was given, and `GRAPH_ID_PATTERN` admits letters in both cases, so a real base64url cursor would have broken on the very first page while every test stayed green. Rule: a value that must round-trip byte-for-byte needs a mixed-case fixture asserted verbatim in **both** directions — inbound Graph→model and outbound model→Graph — and one fixture per direction is not enough if both are written in the same case. Third instance of CC-PROC-53 and the token-shaped one: after a fixture that survived trim/NFKC/truncation and a handle that survived `.toLowerCase()`, this is an identifier that survived `.toUpperCase()`. Closed by `opaque ids and cursors make the round trip byte for byte`, which drives `aB9_x-Media7` and a `QVFIUm…` cursor through both directions | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-55 | A fence has two failure directions, and only the missing-fence direction had ever been tested | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/media.ts`. Wrapping the pager’s own advisory note in `fence(page.note)` survived, because the note was pinned by a single fragment — `assert.match(String(sc.note), /resume from `after`/)` — which the fence leaves intact. That mutant is not cosmetic: the note is **first-party** text the server writes to tell the model how to continue, and fencing it signs it as untrusted Instagram content, which is the exact mirror of the failure the fence exists to prevent. The same sweep found the other direction still open too: `if (m.caption)` in both media record helpers let a **cleared** caption (`caption: ''`, a value Graph really returns and `req` passes through uncast) escape the fence entirely and made “no caption” indistinguishable from “caption deleted” (CC-DATA-2) — a third instance of CC-PROC-46. Rule: every fence site needs assertions in both directions — account-controlled text must be fenced, first-party text must not be — and both need byte-exact pins rather than fragments (CC-PROC-54). Module totals: 125 mutants over `src/tools/media.ts` and `src/tools/index.ts`, 109 killed on the first pass, 16 survivors → 3 real gaps (8 mutants) closed by 2 new tests and 1 tightened assertion, 8 equivalents proven and 3 of them noted in the source. No production defect | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-28 | A loose status match reads the container state as its exact opposite | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/api/publishing.ts`. Seven mutants relaxed the polling loop’s terminal-state comparisons from `code === 'FINISHED'` to `code.includes('FINISHED')` (and `startsWith`, and a `.trim()` on the incoming code) and every one of them survived, even though the test file’s own comment claimed to cover substring matching. It did not: no fixture ever carried a `status_code` that **contains** a known code without being it. The separating values are the ones Graph could plausibly grow — `NOT_FINISHED`, `NOT_EXPIRED`, `UNPUBLISHED`, `NO_ERROR`, `IN_PROGRESS_SLOW`, `NOT_IN_PROGRESS` — and each is the negation or an extension of the state the loose match would read, so the flow would publish a container that just reported it is **not** finished. Rule: a state machine keyed on external string literals is untested until a fixture supplies a near-miss for every literal — the negation, the prefix and the padded spelling — because those are exactly the strings a substring test cannot tell apart from the real one. Closed by extending both polling loops with the near-miss codes, plus `' FINISHED '` for the trim mutant | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-29 | A back-filled id is worse than a missing one: the operator is handed a container id labelled as their publication | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/api/publishing.ts`. Four mutants rewrote what the two writes hand back and all four survived, because no test ever supplied a Graph answer without an `id`: the acknowledgement could be synthesized locally after the `await`, and `mediaId: published.id` could be back-filled as `published.id ?? containerId`. The second is the dangerous one — the caller gets a well-formed id that is not the published media, so every follow-up read, insight and comment call is aimed at the wrong object while the result looks correct. The layer’s contract is pass-through (an absent field is not a false one), so `media_publish` answering without an `id` must produce `mediaId: undefined`, not a plausible substitute. Publishing-side twin of CC-COM-10, and closed the same way: `deepEqual` on the whole acknowledgement plus a case where the ack is silent. The same sweep found the module’s text and URL inputs edited with impunity — `.trim()`, NFKC, truncation, `.split('?')[0]` and `encodeURI` all survived on caption, image, video and cover URLs, location id and user-tag handles, because every fixture was plain ASCII with no query string (CC-PROC-53), and `.split('?')[0]` on a pre-signed URL strips exactly the credentials CC-PUB-8 is about. Module totals: 82 mutants, 50 killed on the first pass, 32 survivors → 31 real gaps closed by 4 new and 6 strengthened tests, 1 proven equivalent. No production defect | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-30 | On a consent gate the intent text is not presentation — it is the thing being consented to | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/publishing.ts`. Six mutants rewrote the `WriteIntent`’s `action` and `summary` for the three composite publishers and for both resume paths, and every one survived: the tests asserted that a preview was produced and that the apply path called Graph, but never what the preview **said**. That is the whole safety property inverted — the operator approves the sentence, so a preview that says “post a reel” while the apply path posts a story is a consent gate that consents to the wrong act, and the resume paths could drop the container id the operator is being asked about. Same failure as the earlier gate whose prompt could name a different account. Rule: every field of a write intent that reaches the operator — `action`, `summary`, and the ids inside them — needs an exact-string assertion per path, not a shape check. Closed by pinning `action` and `summary` for image, carousel, reel and story, and `Resume publishing container <id>` for both resume paths | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-RATE-11 | An exhausted publishing quota is reported as “no data”, because zero is the one value the guard discards | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/publishing.ts`. Relaxing `remaining !== undefined` to plain truthiness survived, and `remaining` is `Math.max(0, quotaTotal - quotaUsage)` — a value this code computes itself, whose single most important reading is exactly `0`. With the mutant, “you have used all 25 posts in this 24 h window” becomes indistinguishable from “Instagram did not tell us the quota”, which is the one answer that invites the model to try publishing anyway. CC-PROC-46 with the sharpest possible instance: the falsy-but-present value is not an oddity Meta might send, it is the state the field exists to report. The same relaxation survived on `status_code: ''` and `status: ''` for the container reads. Rule: where a numeric field is computed rather than passed through, test the boundary value the computation clamps to | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-56 | A refusal that names the wrong field sends the operator to fix an input that was fine | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-09 in `src/tools/publishing.ts`. Five mutants swapped the field NAME inside the https refusals — `assertHttpsUrl(args.coverUrl, 'videoUrl')` and four siblings — and all survived, because the tests asserted only that a validation error was thrown, or matched the message against a fragment that did not include the field. A refusal naming the wrong field is worse than a vague one: the model edits a URL that was already correct, re-submits, and is refused again, with nothing in the loop pointing at the real input. Rule: an error that identifies an input is only tested when a case per input asserts its own name in the message — one assertion cannot cover a family of fields. Module totals for the sweep: 277 mutants over `src/tools/publishing.ts`, 230 killed on the first pass, 47 survivors → 35 real gaps closed and 12 proven equivalents (6 noted in the source, one of them module-wide over the shared-field family, where `httpsUrlSchema` and `graphObjectId()` make a falsy-but-present value unreachable by construction). No production defect | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-27 | `login` asked every operator to grant DM access that no tool in this server can use | Found on 2026-09-10 while auditing `docs/setup-guide.md` §5 against the code. Both default scope sets carried the messaging scope — `instagram_business_manage_messages` on Path A, `instagram_manage_messages` on Path B — and no tool in `allTools` calls a messaging endpoint; M6 messaging is DEFER (`docs/messaging.md`). Three costs, none of them theoretical. The consent screen asks for the most sensitive permission it can ask for, so the operator is invited to decline the whole login over a capability that does not exist. The minted token then carries a standing DM grant for its full 60 days, widening the blast radius of a leak by exactly the surface an attacker most wants. And DM access is on the short list Meta will not approve for an unpublished app (`docs/messaging.md` §App Review), so a scope no code path exercises can fail a review the server does not otherwise need. **Closed 2026-09-10** by dropping it from both defaults; nothing is lost, because `--scopes` passes any scope through verbatim for an operator preparing a messaging build. The guard is a rule, not a list — `no default scope set asks for DM access` filters both arrays on `includes('messages')` — plus a permissive control proving the scope is *unrequested*, not *banned*: `login --scopes=…manage_messages` still reaches the consent screen with it (CC-PROC-50). `docs/setup-guide.md` §5 and `docs/auth.md` §1 now mark it supported-but-not-requested rather than listing it as a default | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-28 | `doctor` printed the granted scopes and stopped, so scope drift was invisible in both directions | Listing a grant is not reviewing it, and nobody reviews seven scope names from memory. `doctor` rendered `Granted scopes: …` and never compared them to anything, which left both failure directions unreported. An **extra** scope is a standing grant the server never exercises — `pages_manage_posts` on a token minted for Instagram reads can write to the Page feed for as long as the token lives. A **missing** scope is worse to diagnose: the token is valid, `doctor` is green, and the gap surfaces weeks later as an opaque Graph permission error at the first `insights` call, looking like a Meta outage. **Closed 2026-09-10** by extracting the table into `src/cli/scopes.ts`, so `login` (what to ask for) and `doctor` (what to say about what was granted) read one contract from two directions. Both verdicts are `warn`, never `fail`, on purpose: a deliberately trimmed grant (`login --scopes=<subset>` for a read-only deployment) is a legitimate setup, and flipping the exit code on it is how a check gets routed around. `public_profile` is exempted by name rather than by pattern, because noise on every single Path B run is how a real finding gets ignored. Path A stays silent — `debug_token` is a Facebook-app endpoint with no `graph.instagram.com` equivalent, so there is no grant to read, and a confident “matches exactly” there would be a lie; the silence is pinned by its own test | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-57 | Two docs promised a capability the code did not have, and the cheap repair was to delete the promise | `docs/setup-guide.md` §5 opened with *“over-granted scopes are flagged by `doctor` so you can trim them”* and `docs/security.md` listed *“`doctor` surfaces … token scope drift so overgranted scopes get trimmed”*. Neither was true (CC-AUTH-28). A docs-drift audit is built to find exactly this, and its default remedy — edit the sentence to match the code — would have been the wrong one here: the sentence described the right behaviour, and the operator reading it was right to want it. The rule this row records: **when a doc and the code disagree, first ask which one is correct.** A promise that the code merely has not kept yet is a specification, and deleting it destroys the only written record that anyone wanted the feature; only a promise that is genuinely wrong about the design should be rewritten. Closed 2026-09-10 by implementing the capability instead, which made both sentences true without editing either. Companion to CC-PROC-46 (presence is not truth): a doc claim is not evidence that the code does it, in either direction | post-M5 · doc |
| CC-AUTH-29 | `debugToken` dropped every falsy-but-present introspection field, and no fixture in the tree had one | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/account.ts`. Rewriting any mapping line of the `debug_token` response as `\|\| undefined` — `data_access_expires_at: 0`, `scopes: []`, and at api level `is_valid: false` and `expires_at: 0` — survived, because the only fixture with a `data` envelope spelled every field in its truthy form and `data_access_expires_at: 0` / `scopes: []` were supplied by no test anywhere. The consequence is a misdiagnosis in the direction `doctor` exists to catch: a token whose data access never expires is reported as one whose data-access expiry is *unknown*, and a token carrying **no scopes at all** — a broken grant — is reported exactly like a Path A token whose scopes were never inspected, so CC-AUTH-28's drift verdict has nothing to compare and stays silent on the one grant that most needs a verdict. Fifth instance of CC-PROC-46 and the credential-shaped one: `0` and `[]` are answers, not absences. Closed by `debugToken passes falsy-but-present introspection fields through as themselves (CC-PROC-46)`, a whole-object `deepEqual` that also chains `expiresAtSec: 0` into `summarizeTokenExpiry` → `{ state: 'never' }` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-26 | A brand-new account has zero followers and zero posts, and both readers rendered zero as “unavailable” | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/account.ts` and `src/api/discovery.ts`. `wire.followers_count \|\| undefined` and `wire.media_count \|\| undefined` survived the full suite in `getAccount`, and the same pair plus `follows_count` in `discoverBusiness`, because every fixture carries either populated counts (`1200`, `87`) or omits the field — no test in the tree had ever seen a `0`. The documented meaning of an absent count is “Graph withheld it”, so a fresh professional account and a competitor with no followers were both reported as accounts whose numbers could not be read, and `discoverBusiness` has no `notes`/`truncated` channel that could have flagged the difference. Empty strings (`biography`, `website`, `name`) fell the same way. Closed by `getAccount passes falsy-but-present profile values through as themselves (CC-PROC-46)` and `discoverBusiness passes falsy-but-present counts and an empty media edge through as themselves (CC-PROC-46)`, both whole-object comparisons with every count `0` and every string `''`. The tools layer had the same hole one floor up (`profile.followersCount \|\| undefined` in `src/tools/account.ts` survived too), closed by `get_account reports a zero follower/following/media count, not an absent one` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-27 | A disclosed-but-empty edge is an empty array, not a missing key | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/media.ts`. Changing the guard `if (children?.data)` to `if (children?.data?.length)` survived the full suite: the tests pinned the absent key, `children: {}` and `children: { data: null }` (all three must yield no key) and a two-child album, but never `children: { data: [] }`. The distinction matters because the key is the answer to a question: an album whose every child has since been deleted is still a carousel, and the `'children' in detail` test a caller uses to ask “is this a carousel?” answered *no* for one. The same guard on the `media` edge in `discoverBusiness` had the same hole (an account whose media edge Graph disclosed and found empty became one whose edge was withheld), while the identical guard in `comments.ts` was already pinned by `replies: { data: [] }` fixtures — asymmetry between neighbouring modules again (CC-INS-9). Rule: an edge that can be absent, null, empty or populated needs all four spellings in the fixture set, and the empty one asserted as `[]` by whole-object comparison. Closed by `getMedia keeps an empty children array as an empty array, not as an absent edge (CC-PROC-46)` and the discovery test of CC-DATA-26 | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-58 | An api mapper whose fixtures are all lower-case, trimmed ASCII cannot tell a transport from an editor | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/account.ts`. `.trim()` and `.toLowerCase()` inserted on `username`, on the `input_token` param and on `igId` before `encodeURIComponent`, and `\|\| undefined` on the linked-account id, all survived the full suite: every string fixture in the file (`acme`, `EAAsecret`, `178414`, `page1`, `ig1`) is a fixed point of every one of those edits, and the path-injection sweep's hostile ids are none of them whitespace-edged. The api layer is a mapper, not an editor, and each survivor is a different lie — a trimmed `input_token` introspects a *different credential* than the one `doctor` was asked about, and an edited linked `igId` is the id the operator then writes into their config. Fourth instance of CC-PROC-53, closed differently: rather than one more hand-picked fixture, three fast-check properties over a `nonFixedPointString` arbitrary that always draws ` padded `, `\tTabbed\n`, `ﬁne` (NFKC-unstable), `MixedCase`, `a?b=c`, `''` and `' '` plus arbitrary graphemes, asserting the request options and the result whole (`getAccount property — every profile field and the igId travel verbatim, never normalised`, `listLinkedAccounts property — …`, `debugToken property — the inspected token is sent verbatim, never normalised`). Rule: for a verbatim contract, generate the counter-fixtures instead of enumerating them | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-INS-10 | The retention guard treated `since: 0` and `until: 0` as “no bound” | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/insights.ts`. Rewriting `since !== undefined` / `until !== undefined` as plain truthiness — in the outer gate and in both inner guards — survived the full suite, because every retention fixture is a large positive epoch (`NOW_SEC - n * DAY`). The tool schema admits `0` (`z.number().int().optional()`), so the epoch reaches this layer as a real bound: `until: 0` is a window that ends in 1970 and should be refused locally, but under the mutant it is forwarded — a rate-limited call spent on a range that cannot hold data, answered with a silent empty result, the exact failure CC-INS-3 exists to prevent — and `since: 0` (“from the beginning”) is forwarded verbatim with `clamped: false` and no note, so Graph truncates to 90 days and the model is told it received the range it asked for. Sixth instance of CC-PROC-46, on an input rather than an output. Closed by `getAccountInsights treats since: 0 and until: 0 as present bounds, not as absent ones (CC-PROC-46)`, pinning the whole refusal for `until: 0` and the whole clamp (`window`, `notes`, request options) for `since: 0` | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-INS-11 | A `media_product_type` that merely resembles a known one was judged by the look-alike's vocabulary | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/insights.ts`. Rewriting the `MEDIA_METRIC_MATRIX` lookup as a prefix match, a substring match, or a trim-then-match survived the full suite, because the fixtures use the exact keys (`story`, `FEED`, `REELS`) and one wholly different string (`NEW_META_TYPE`) — nothing that shares a prefix with a key or carries edge whitespace. The exact match is what protects CC-DATA-6's open-vocabulary rule: an unknown type must *spend the call* and let Meta rule on the metrics, so a future `STORY_HIGHLIGHT` or `FEED_AD` judged by `STORY`'s or `FEED`'s list would be refused metrics Graph would have accepted. Rule 7 of the third pass: every literal comparison needs a near-miss fixture that shares a prefix, a substring, and a whitespace edge with the literal. Closed by `a near-miss media_product_type is an unknown type, not the type it resembles (CC-DATA-6)`, ten near misses each required to pass `['likes', 'navigation']` unrefused to the seam | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-31 | The https check had never met a scheme that merely *begins with* `https` | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/media-spec.ts`. `new URL(value).protocol === 'https:'` rewritten as `.startsWith('https')` or `.includes('https')` survived the full suite: the fixtures were `http://`, `ftp://`, garbage, `''`, `HTTPS://`, `https:/` and `https://` — every non-https scheme a completely different word. The WHATWG parser accepts any well-formed scheme, so `new URL('httpsx://cdn/a.jpg').protocol` is `httpsx:` and under the loose comparison `httpsx://`, `https2://`, `httpss://`, `https-cdn://` and `xhttps://` all pass `isHttpsUrl`, `assertHttpsUrl` and `httpsUrlSchema` — the SSRF control of `docs/security.md` forwarding a non-https URL to Graph on a verdict it never earned. Not a live defect (the production comparison is exact), but a security control with no adversarial fixture is one refactor away from being one. Closed by `a scheme that merely begins with or contains "https" is not https`, five schemes through all three entry points, each refusal compared whole | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-32 | A container status that *begins with* a terminal code was never supplied, so a prefix match looked exact | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/api/publishing.ts`. | CC-RATE-13 | A usage reading of **0%** was indistinguishable from "no usage header" | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/core/http.ts` (`parseUsage`). Three survivors on the one function: relaxing `if (appUsagePct !== undefined)` and `if (bucUsagePct !== undefined)` to truthiness, and the `present` filter from `n !== undefined` to `n`. Every usage fixture carried a non-zero percentage (25 / 40 / 95), so a fresh app at the top of its window — `call_count: 0`, the honest reading right after the window rolls — was never on the wire, and a truthy guard drops `appUsagePct: 0`, `bucUsagePct: 0` and, through the filter, `maxPct: 0`. The consumer (the `doctor` verdict, the account tool's usage panel) then cannot tell "quiet" from "no telemetry": a `maxPct === undefined` snapshot reads as a missing header. The same rule as CC-RATE-11 (an exhausted quota is `0`) seen from the other end of the window: zero is the one value the guard discards, and it is a value this code computes itself. Closed by `a usage reading of 0% is a reading, not a missing header — the snapshot carries the zeros` (`test/core/http.test.ts`: whole-snapshot `deepEqual` including `raw`, no sleep, no warn). No production change | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-RATE-14 | A retry schedule that stopped doubling was invisible: the first two backoffs of `500·2^n` and `500·(n+1)` are the same numbers | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/core/http.ts` (`backoffMs`). Rewriting the exponential to a linear schedule survived the whole suite because 500 and 1000 ms are the first two values of **both** formulas, and every retry test observed at most two sleeps; only the third (2000 vs 1500 ms) separates them, and the `[base, 1.5·base)` jitter bands of the two overlap there, so even a three-retry test asserting an interval could pass by luck. A fixed-point fixture (CC-PROC-53) with two coinciding points: the schedule `docs/operations.md` §2 promises (`min(500·2^n, 8000)` + jitter) had no test that could see it stop growing. Closed by `the third backoff doubles again — 500, 1000, 2000 ms with the jitter pinned to zero` (`Math.random` mocked to 0 via `t.mock.method`, three transport failures, `deepEqual(clock.sleeps, [500, 1000, 2000])`). No production change | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-RATE-15 | `Retry-After: 2s` read as two seconds, `Retry-After:  2 ` read through the wrong branch | Found by mutation testing 2026-09-18 in `src/core/http.ts` (`parseRetryAfter`). The prefix grammar honours `'2s'` as 2000 ms — a value the server never sent — instead of falling back to the backoff band; the un-trimmed variant routes `' 2 '` through `Number(' 2 ')` on a branch the `Date.parse` fixture happened to cover. Near-miss literals (rule 7) for a header whose only tested spellings were the well-formed ones. Closed by `a padded delta-seconds Retry-After is trimmed when the transport does not normalize` and the extended `an unparseable or blank Retry-After falls back to exponential backoff` (`'2s'`, `'5;foo'`, `'5 seconds'` each asserted to land in the backoff band).
