The fix is the same in both places — scrape the source array and compare it order-sensitively; loop over both branches — and the rule is that a name saying *every* must derive its population from the thing under test ([[CC-PROC-127]], [[CC-PROC-118]]). | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-131 | Two release channels publish the same configuration surface, a gate is written for one of them, and the sibling keeps the defect the gate exists to catch. | When a property is worth a gate on one channel, port it — the channels differ in packaging, not in what an operator can be told. Measured 2026-09-23: `test/release/plugin-manifest.test.ts` walked every string of `.claude-plugin/*` twice, for `IG_*` names the server never reads ([[CC-PROC-17]]’s `IG_FB_ACCESS_TOKEN` defect) and for token-shaped values in a committed artifact. `test/release/mcpb-manifest.test.ts` had neither, although its own docstring argues the MCPB channel is the worse place for the first — *“it comes with a title, a description and a text box that look authoritative”*. It checked env-block KEYS only; the six `user_config` prose fields the operator reads while deciding what to paste were scanned by nothing, `:378` asserting only that a description is a non-empty string and the two content scans below it matching lowercase tokens. Both drifts were planted and both were silent before the port and killed after ([[CC-PROC-112]]). A per-channel test file makes this invisible on a read: the absent gate looks like a file that simply ends. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-132 | A source comment cites a test by title as the evidence for the line it guards — *“Pinned by …”* — and that title is a string in a comment that nothing resolves. | Make the citation an assertion. Measured 2026-09-23: eleven notes in `src/` cite a test by title across seven files — four in `core/refresh.ts` alone, two each in `cli/login.ts`, `core/config-write.ts` and `tools/publishing.ts` — and all eleven resolved, but nothing in `test/**` scraped them. A rename or a deletion on the other side leaves a note that still reads as a measurement while naming nothing, and the next reader cannot tell a renamed test from a deleted guard — the failure mode [[CC-PROC-118]] describes, one layer up: the note claims a kill it can no longer produce. `test/source-citations.test.ts` now scrapes the suite’s 1907 `test(` and `describe(` titles and demands an exact match per citation, printing the closest surviving title when one is missing. Two drifts were planted and both killed it ([[CC-PROC-112]]): renaming a cited test in `test/cli/doctor.test.ts`, and stripping the citation verbs from `src/`, which fires the gate’s own empty-set arm — a title scrape passes vacuously the moment its pattern stops matching, so it has to refuse a zero count. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-36 | A dependency is configured at the call site — `quiet: true` — and resolves that same switch from the ENVIRONMENT first, so an operator’s shell silently overrules the code. | Withdraw the knob; passing the option is not enough. Measured 2026-09-23 against dotenv 17.4.2: `configDotenv` resolves both of its switches as `parseBoolean(processEnv.DOTENV_CONFIG_QUIET \|\| (options && options.quiet))` — the environment operand is on the LEFT and wins. `DOTENV_CONFIG_QUIET` set to any of `false`, `0`, `no`, `off` or the empty string therefore brings the load banner back, and `DOTENV_CONFIG_DEBUG` adds a trace per candidate naming the config-home path, both on stdout — the one stream the stdio transport frames as JSON-RPC — while `loadEnvFiles` is still passing `quiet: true` and reading as correct. These are dotenv’s own documented switches, exported for a whole shell rather than one command, and the entry harness hands the ambient environment to the child untouched, so a developer with either one set would have watched the existing stdout-purity test go red with no hint that the cause was their shell. `src/index.ts` now deletes both keys for the length of the load and restores them exactly, unset included — `''` parses as false, so restoring a missing knob by assignment would turn quiet OFF for whoever reads it next. The planted control, a withdrawal made into a no-op, put `┆ no encoding is specified (UTF-8 is used by default)` on line 0 of stdout and killed only the new test ([[CC-PROC-112]], [[CC-PROC-93]]). | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-133 | A gate flattens line breaks before it matches, and the census deciding whether that gate is complete is taken with line-based `grep`. | Enumerate with a port of the gate’s own pattern, never with `grep`. `test/source-citations.test.ts` collapses a newline and its `*` or `//` furniture before scraping, so a citation wrapping mid-phrase is one string to the gate and two invisible halves to a line matcher. Measured 2026-09-23: a control that reworded every path citation `grep` could see left the gate’s empty-set arm unfired — a correct negative that read exactly like a dead gate — because `docs/architecture.md:117` wraps `pinned by` and its backticked path across the break. A Python port of the same regex found it immediately, and the rewritten control then fired. The rule generalises past this file: when a test normalises its input before matching, the only trustworthy inventory is one that normalises the same way, and every other count is a claim ([[CC-PROC-109]]) about a population nobody read. It is [[CC-PROC-129]] turned on the measuring tool rather than on the test. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-134 | An equivalent-mutant note names the exact input that WOULD separate the two forms, and then argues that this input never arrives. | Read a named counterexample as an unwritten test whenever the harness can supply it. `src/index.ts` argued that dropping `Number.isFinite(major)` changes nothing, since `NaN < 22` is false either way, and named `-Infinity` itself as the string that fails the finite test while satisfying the comparison — so the bare form REFUSES it. Production cannot produce one, because only Node writes `process.versions.node`; the suite can, because `test/helpers/entry-preload.ts` already defines `IG_TEST_FAKE_NODE_VERSION` over that property and the two tests using it passed only `20.11.0` and `24.11.0`. The rule the guard exists to state — tolerate what we cannot parse — was therefore held by nothing, and losing it stops the server on a version string it merely failed to read. Measured 2026-09-23: the bare comparison passed 39 of the 40 entry tests and was killed only by the new one. A note that can name its own counterexample has already done the hard half; what is left is six lines ([[CC-PROC-92]]). | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-135 | A gate scrapes this project’s `IG_*` namespace with an unanchored pattern, so a foreign identifier that merely ends in one — `DOTENV_CONFIG_DEBUG`, `XDG_CONFIG_HOME` — is read as `IG_DEBUG` or `IG_HOME`. | Anchor the token with a lookbehind. The false accusation is the loud half; the quiet half is worse — the same scrape also builds the gate’s allowlist, where a foreign name enters as “known” and lets a genuine phantom through, so both halves must use the anchored form. A settled spelling elsewhere in the suite is the fix, not a new invention (see [[CC-PROC-34]]). | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-136 | A suite whose coverage gate is `--check-coverage --statements=100` gains a test that spawns a repo script, and the spawned script joins the report it was never written to satisfy. | Detach the child by setting `NODE_V8_COVERAGE` to the EMPTY STRING — not by deleting it. Under `npm run coverage` c8 exports `NODE_V8_COVERAGE` into the environment, and `scripts/**` is in none of c8’s default excludes, so a child that merely runs lands in the report on its own statements. Measured 2026-09-23 while writing `test/release/live-probe-plan.test.ts`, which spawns `scripts/live-probe.mjs` eleven times: the script entered the report at **44.84 %** statements and took the whole gate down with it, on a file no test claims to cover. The obvious fix is measured WRONG — deleting the variable from the child’s `env` does nothing, because Node re-injects it into every descendant for as long as the coverage session is live; the control that proves it is an ordinary variable removed the same way, which does disappear. The empty string is what detaches: verified that nothing is written anywhere (`coverage/tmp` 120 files before and after, repo root unchanged, no stray `coverage-*.json`) and the script leaves the report entirely. The general shape — a coverage session is inherited, so a test that spawns anything decides what that thing is measured as — outlives this repo’s c8 version, and the planned mitigation that had to be thrown away is [[CC-PROC-109]] applied to one’s own note. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-137 | A docs gate walks its subject with a non-recursive `readdirSync`, or globs one tree while its subject spans two, so the scope it governs is whatever the scrape happened to reach rather than anything anyone chose. | Make the exclusion explicit and assert BOTH directions. Measured 2026-09-23: `test/docs-design.test.ts` read `docs/` non-recursively and left the seven files of `docs/reviews/` outside every gate in the file while its own docstring claimed it governed “every markdown file the project publishes”; `test/source-citations.test.ts` scraped `src/**` and `docs/**` only, leaving three live citations inside `test/**` unwatched; and `test/docs-user-guides.test.ts` swept `src/cli/doctor.ts` for `'--flag'` literals and matched zero — `doctor` parses no arguments at all, so the sweep looked like coverage while adding none. The repair in each case is a named exemption table (`UNGOVERNED_DOC_TREES`) plus two assertions: nothing unexempted escapes, and every exemption still names something real. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-138 | A version range is parsed as `the first run of digits`, so the parser answers confidently for shapes it cannot actually mean. | Anchor the whole range and refuse what the anchor does not admit. `test/release/launcher.test.ts` took a major with `/(\d+)/`, which reads `>=22.12` as 22 — a floor below the granularity the launcher shim can enforce, so the shim would admit a runtime the range excludes — and reads an alternation range (`18.x` OR `>=20`, joined by the npm union operator) as 18, the LOWEST major in the union rather than the floor. Measured 2026-09-23 with a positive control: the pre-fix parser plus `engines.node` set to `">=22.12"` ran `# pass 5 # fail 0`, so the old spelling was blind to precisely the case it existed to catch. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-139 | A gate keeps an exemption table and asserts things ABOUT its entries, but never that each entry still matches anything real; and one entry holds a value that looks derivable from `package.json`. | Assert the second direction, and derive from the ledger that records history rather than the file that records today. `test/docs-design.test.ts` exempted the literal `v0.7.0` as “this package’s own release tag”: stale from the next bump onward, with nothing failing when an entry stopped matching. Deriving it from `package.json` would have been the wrong repair, because documents carry history that stays true — `docs/roadmap.md` and `docs/workplan.md` record the 2026-08-25 publication of `v0.7.0` as a dated fact, and a current-version check would have begun accusing two correct records. The tags now come from `CHANGELOG.md`, the ledger of every version actually shipped, which also fails a document naming a tag that never went out. Measured 2026-09-23: control 8/8; a stale entry, an unreadable ledger, and a doc naming an unshipped `v0.9.9` each killed a DIFFERENT assertion; and on a simulated 0.8.0 bump the pre-fix gate failed while the fixed gate passed. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-140 | An exemption is keyed on the token alone, although the token’s meaning depends on WHICH document it appears in, so exempting it disarms the rule everywhere else the rule was doing real work. | Exempt the position, not the name. `test/docs-user-guides.test.ts` governed three operator guides and silently omitted the fourth, `docs/plugin-install.md` — the sibling install route, same operator, same decision — so nothing checked the env names, tool names, npm scripts or flags it spells. Bringing it in exposed two names that mean opposite things in different guides. `--scope` is Claude Code’s own selector there, but in `docs/setup-guide.md` §7 it is the near miss `login` refuses with exit 2, showable only inside that refusal; a blanket foreign-flag entry would have switched §7’s rule off for every guide at once, so the table now records WHICH guide each exemption is for. A token variable no code reads is named in the new guide only to DENY that it exists, so the allowance is a required phrase on the same line rather than a pass for the name — the same positional shape the rejected-flag rule already had. Measured 2026-09-23: control 5/5; seven mutants each killed exactly one assertion (a phantom env var, a renamed tool, a missing npm script, an undeclared flag, a vanished exemption target, a softened denial, and `--scope` planted in a guide it is not foreign in); the identical four mutations planted in ungoverned `docs/operations.md` passed 5/5, proving the kills come from the new guide’s membership; and with the three new reverse assertions stripped the vanished-exemption mutant passed too. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-141 | Every loop in a gate walks a block that can be empty, and the helper guarding that block refuses only a non-object, so `{}` sails through; the bijection meant to pin the two halves together is `deepEqual([], [])`, the happiest assertion there is. The gate is then vacuous as a whole, not in one branch. | Give the walk a floor, and derive the number rather than counting it. Dropping the four credential and identity fields from BOTH halves of `manifest.json` left `test/release/mcpb-manifest.test.ts` green at 15/15 while Claude Desktop rendered a form that collects no credential at all. The floor is `Object.keys(PROFILE_ENV)` united with `VERIFIED_DEFAULTS` — every variable `core/config.ts` reads to resolve a profile, for which a bundle has no `.env` to fall back on. Two tidier-looking derivations were measured and rejected: parity with `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` (one userConfig key against six) and a bijection with `.env.example` (22 keys, of which MCPB deliberately exposes a subset). | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-142 | A lockstep check sits behind a chain of nested `if`s — a non-array, an empty array, an entry without the key — and each one skips the assertion in silence. Every skip is a place the gate decided to say nothing without anyone choosing it. | Turn each skip into a decision the gate states out loud. Deleting the single line `"version"` from `server.json`, and separately emptying its `packages[]`, both left `test/release/version-consistency.test.ts` green at 8/8 while the published registry entry stopped pinning a version at all; both now fail with the reason named. The field being OPTIONAL in the upstream schema is exactly why its absence has to be deliberate, not inferred. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-143 | A gate asserts that a BUILT artifact exists, so it cannot be measured inside a snapshot whose rsync excludes `dist/`. The lane then carries a baseline failure, and every kill count in it is read against a number nobody has explained. | Explain the baseline before trusting the lane, then remove it. A `# fail 1` that appeared in CONTROL and RESTORED alike was `manifest.json` naming `entry_point` under `dist/`; stubbing that one path in the snapshot returned the control to 17 pass / 0 fail. Only then did the headline mutant read correctly: not “one failure before, two after” but zero before — the file had been wholly vacuous — and exactly one after, named. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-144 | The obvious second direction for a one-directional exemption set checks each entry against the SUBJECT, but what the entry claims is about the GATE: “a test in this file verifies this key’s default”. So the obvious check is redundant with tests that already read the subject, and the staleness it was written for walks straight past it. | Check the claim against the only thing that can falsify it. Removing `user_config.IG_TOOL_PACKAGES.default` was already killed before the repair, so asserting the default still exists added nothing; deleting the TEST that verifies it while leaving the key listed survived at 15 pass / 0 fail, and is now caught by scanning the gate’s own source for the expression that reads that default back — the shape `test/env-catalog.test.ts` already used to prune `NOT_ENV_VARS`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-145 | A measurement harness written in zsh keeps its rsync flags in a scalar and its fidelity guard on the emptiness of `diff`’s stdout. zsh does not word-split an unquoted scalar, so the flags arrive as one argument and the copy never happens; `diff` then errors to STDERR, leaving stdout empty, and the guard reports a perfect snapshot of a directory that does not exist. | Flag lists are arrays, and a guard tests an exit code rather than an absence of output. The broken lane printed `SNAP_FIDELITY_OK` and went on to three builds that all failed, against a snapshot that was never created — a harness bug that, in the other direction, would have printed a clean mutation score just as confidently. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-146 | One helper parses a document into rows and several gates share it, so its result is the floor under all of them at once. It comes back empty when a heading it slices on is reordered above the table, and SHORT — quietly dropping just the rows it stopped understanding — when the row format drifts. Every caller states per-row claims, which both cases satisfy for free. | Two floors, because there are two failures. Emptiness is the whole of the first, and a table that only grows admits no threshold above zero that would not be arbitrary; completeness is the second, and it is what a partial drift trips while the count still looks healthy. In `test/docs-catalog.test.ts` both mutants passed at 6 / 0 before the repair: moving the first register heading above the table, and adding a row whose ID was hand-typed in lowercase, each left the duplicate-ID, column-count and section-placement gates walking nothing and reporting clean. The same file already carried the idiom one test lower — `sites > 400`, under the comment that a guard which scans nothing passes vacuously. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-147 | A file pins which tool arguments carry the Graph object-id rule and asserts the pin in BOTH directions: every listed field carries the rule, and no unlisted field does. Both sweeps read the same schemas, so they agree with each other about an argument that was never declared with the id helper and never listed — it is absent from both sides and neither sweep says a word. The docstring claimed the opposite. | Derive a third direction from the one piece of evidence the omission cannot erase: the argument’s NAME. Measure the convention on the surface first — all 19 arguments named like an id already carry the rule, with no exemption needed, so it is exact rather than approximate, and the regex encodes the house spelling (`mediaId`, `media_id`, bare `id`) instead of `/id$/i`, which would also claim `valid`. The one id field whose name says nothing (`children`, an array of container ids) stays covered by the bidirectional sweep and is pinned as the single expected exception, which doubles as the floor. A new id argument added as a bare string and integrated exactly as a developer would integrate it — described, key set re-pinned, published contract re-pinned — survived 460 of 460 tests across the whole tool surface, docs-sync and the entry test before the repair, and fails by name after it. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-148 | An audit flags a one-directional exemption list by shape: tools that deliberately declare no log fields “because their input carries nothing worth logging”, with nothing in that file re-checking the justification. Read as a hole, it is the same shape as a real one. | Run the mutant before repairing it. Adding an argument to an exempted tool dies in four other gates — the per-tool argument-key pin, the “no arguments at all” pin, the byte-for-byte published-contract pin and the described-fields sweep — so the claim the list makes is held from outside and the list itself needs nothing. Shape is where to look; only a measurement decides, and the cost of the wrong verdict is a redundant assertion that dilutes the next real finding. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-149 | A test inspects every refusal a module raises and pins the whole error record field by field, so a fabricated Graph `code` cannot be smuggled in beside `kind` and handed to the model as if an upstream had said it. The list of sites is written by hand — only an invocation produces an error to look at — and nothing derives it from the module, so the sweep is a claim about five throw sites that the module itself never checks. | Count the throws in the module’s own source and make the hand list answer to that count. The anchor is a line that BEGINS with the throw, so the `{@link}` references in the docstrings above them cannot inflate it. A sixth refusal added exactly the way a developer would add one — a constant, an exported validator, its own passing unit test — and throwing `{ kind: 'validation', code: 2207026 }` survived all 1310 tests before the repair and fails at the count after it, alone. No separate vacuity floor is needed: the counted side is read from the file, so a pattern that stops matching reads 0 and the equality fails on the spot — confirmed by degrading the pattern and watching it fire. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-150 | A drift guard scans the api sources for request paths that are not plain literals and requires each to be exercised by a pinned call, in both directions. The scan knows exactly one spelling, the `path:` property. A path built into a local and handed to the descriptor as shorthand is not reported as a gap — it is simply absent — so a whole new function goes unswept while both directions stay green. | Teach the scan the second spelling, then stop enumerating spellings and count. Every request descriptor in this layer carries a `method`, which cannot be omitted or built elsewhere, so it counts the descriptors, and every descriptor owes the scan exactly one path it can read. A new exported api function with a declared path and shorthand survived 1309 of 1309 tests before the repair; after it the guard names the function, and with the new pattern degraded the count floor names the file (“media.ts: 4 request descriptors but 3 readable request paths”). The reverse floor the audit proposed — assert the scan found something — was already held by the guard’s own stale direction: a pattern that matches nothing turns all 23 pinned entries stale at once. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-151 | Two gates sweep `test/fixtures/` for secrets and for synthetic-looking content, and both are per-fixture loops over `listFixtures()`. That helper does one non-recursive `readdirSync` and keeps the names ending in `.json`, so a capture committed one directory down, or under any other extension, is never reported as unscanned — it is simply absent from the list, and every loop walks past it without a word. An empty directory reads the same way: both gates state a per-fixture claim, and a corpus of nothing satisfies them for free. | **Closed 2026-09-23** with `every file under test/fixtures is one the gates below can actually read`, which reads the directory itself rather than the helper's view of it, names any file `listFixtures()` cannot see, and floors the list as non-empty. Dotfiles are exempted as editor or operating-system furniture; a second assertion stops that exemption becoming a hiding place by refusing a dotfile that is also a fixture. Measured: a `test/fixtures/graph/list-media.json` carrying a real-looking handle and caption survived 33 of 33 tests before the repair, and after it the new gate names the file. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-152 | The fixture policy gate judged only the keys `DEFAULT_FIELD_POLICY` names. The sanitizer is default-deny — a field it does not recognise is dropped rather than copied through, because an unrecognised field is precisely where a handle or a token hides — so judging only the policied keys inverted the sanitizer's strictest case into the gate's one blind spot, and a hand-written fixture never passed through the sanitizer at all. Separately, each of the three `rule ===` branches is a conditional over a committed corpus: until a fixture carried a handle, the handle branch was unreachable and deleting it would have cost nothing. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The gate is default-deny itself now: a key in no policy fails unless it is the documented `_`-prefixed provenance marker, written as a rule so a second marker needs no edit. A branch-count pin scraped out of the test file's own source (the regex spells the parenthesis escaped, so it cannot match itself) is compared against the named rule list, and each named rule must be exercised by some committed fixture. Measured, each alone: an un-policied `handle` holding a real-looking value survived the pre-fix file and is named by the post-fix one; deleting `test/fixtures/example-list-comments.json`, the only fixture carrying a `username`, fires the coverage assertion; a fourth `rule ===` branch fires the count pin, which proves the scrape reads source rather than a constant. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-153 | The catalogue drift tests slice this document at the first register heading, and that boundary was the literal `## 9.` — a fact about today's numbering rather than about the document. Split a themed section and renumber, and the boundary resolves to a heading in the middle of the main table: the slice ends hundreds of rows early, every per-row gate walks the surviving prefix, and both floors still read healthy because the truncated list is neither empty nor unparseable. Nothing reports the rows that fell off the end. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The boundary is derived from what actually distinguishes the two halves — a themed section advertises the prefix it collects, a register does not — and an ordering claim keeps that derivation from acquiring the same blind spot. Headings alone would have introduced a new one: a trailing themed heading that loses its `(CC-XXX)` suffix truncates the table silently and still satisfies the ordering claim, so a structural gate was added on top. The main table carries four columns and the registers three, so a four-column row below the boundary is a main-table row nothing above checked. Measured: shifting every section number by one, plus a duplicate `CC-PROC-4` row injected into the old §8, survived 6 of 6 tests with roughly 250 rows outside every gate, and fails on the duplicate after the repair. §8 losing its suffix fires the four-column gate; §3 losing its suffix, and a register growing one, each fire the ordering claim. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-154 | A gate walks the §3 code-to-kind table in `docs/operations.md` and `continue`s past any row whose mapping cell it cannot parse, then guards itself with a floor: at least ten rows were checked. Both halves are blind to the same row. One that merely lost the backticks around `kind: x` reads as having no mapping, takes the skip, and sits outside every assertion in the file while the document goes on stating a mapping for it — and a floor of ten against an actual thirteen leaves three rows of slack for exactly that to happen in. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The skip is named rather than counted: a row with neither a `kind` nor a subcode column fails on the spot, because a subcode row is the only one that earns the skip honestly and the subcode test below it is what holds it. The floor is replaced by an equality against the classifier itself — the `code === n` rungs and the one band are scraped out of `src/core/errors.ts`, the codes a subcode row carries are subtracted, and what the loop walked must be exactly what `deriveKind` decides on. A rung §3 does not name is a classification no operator can look up; a §3 entry no rung answers to is a promise about a code that in fact falls through to the default. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-155 | The README profile table is parsed by a row regex that captures the profile name and the tool count but steps over the middle column with a negated character class. That column is the one cell an operator actually copies — the literal pasted into `IG_TOOL_PACKAGES` — so its value was read and thrown away. A wrong list is the quieter of the two failures the table can carry: it is still a valid explicit selection, so the server starts and simply exposes a different surface than the row promised. The table’s own floor was `size > 0`, which lets a row go quiet. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The middle column is captured and compared against `PACKAGE_PROFILES` for every row that spells a literal list, and the floor is replaced by set equality between the table’s profiles and the registry’s plus `all` — a row naming a profile the registry does not define is not a documentation slip, `selectPackages` falls through to its explicit-list branch and throws at startup, so the operator who followed the table cannot start the server at all. Which rows carry a literal list is asserted rather than assumed, because `all` spells its value in prose: a list that decayed into prose would otherwise be checked by nothing. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-156 | `SINK_GONE_CODES` is module-private in `src/core/log.ts` on purpose — it is an implementation detail, not part of the logger’s contract — so the tests that walk it carried a hand-written duplicate. A hand copy pins in one direction only: dropping a member turns the loops red, ADDING one is invisible. The cost of that gap is not symmetric with its size, because `isSinkGone` feeds a permanent latch. A fifth code — `EAGAIN` on a non-blocking pipe, say — turns a condition that clears by itself into the end of logging for the life of the process, and the only negative control the file carries is `ENOSPC`. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The list is scraped out of the module’s own source, with two floors that fire before the loops do: the set literal must still parse, and it must parse to at least one code. A scrape that stops matching therefore fails by name instead of quietly emptying every loop that stands on it. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-157 | A loop that must cover EVERY member of a union listed the members under an `ErrorKind[]` annotation. That checks membership, not exhaustiveness: a sixth kind added to `core/types.ts` compiles against it and is simply never probed. What the loop pins is `isError`, the single bit telling a client that a call did not do what it says, and nothing else in the suite re-derives that bit per kind — so the unprobed kind hands the model a failure dressed as data, which for a write tool means a retry loop against a request that may already have landed. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The list is written as an object literal `satisfies Record<ErrorKind, true>`, so a kind added to the union fails the BUILD until this loop covers it. The type checker is the right channel for an exhaustiveness claim: the runtime alternative is a count, and a count has to be told the number — which is the thing the next edit forgets. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-158 | A gate inspects every refusal a module raises and pins each error record whole, and answers to a count scraped out of the module’s source so the hand-written list of sites cannot fall behind. The anchor was a line that BEGINS with the throw, chosen to keep the `{@link InstagramError}` references in the docstrings out of the count. It is blind to the single-line `if (cond) throw new InstagramError(...)` form — a form this repo already writes for `return`, and one no lint rule here forbids, since `curly` and `brace-style` are both unconfigured in `eslint.config.js`. A sixth refusal written that way leaves the count at five, the equality still holds, and the new site’s error record is audited by nothing. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** Comments are stripped rather than dodged with a line anchor, and the keyword is then counted anywhere on a line. The anchor was never what kept the docstrings out either: they say `Throw {@link InstagramError}`, which carries neither the lowercase keyword nor the `new`. Measured: a sixth refusal added as a one-line `if (...) throw new InstagramError(..., { kind: 'validation', code: 2207026 })` survived all 1939 tests of the suite before the repair and fails the count after it. The same rule in block form was killed on both sides, which is the positive control that the channel was live rather than merely quiet. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-159 | Two manifests kept in step by hand are compared field by field with `assert.equal(a[f], b[f])`. Equality alone is satisfied by `undefined === undefined`, so the edit that actually happens — the field dropped from both files at once, because whoever edits one edits the other — is exactly the edit the comparison cannot see. The four fields are the whole of what a plugin install listing shows before anything is fetched. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** Presence is asserted on BOTH sides before the two are compared: each field must be a non-empty string in `.claude-plugin/plugin.json` and in the `marketplace.json` entry, and only then must the two be equal. Measured: deleting `"repository"` from both manifests survived all 1939 tests before the repair — a listing that had stopped saying where the code it is about to run comes from — and is named after it. Deleting it from the marketplace entry alone was killed on both sides, which is the positive control. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-160 | The two claim-gates over the documentation — every Graph API version named must be the pinned one, every Graph host named must be one the SSRF allowlist admits — enumerated their pages with a `.md` filter. `docs/index.html` is the landing page: the document the most people read and the fewest people edit, stating the pin four times and naming the Graph hosts twelve times in the same claiming voice as the markdown, and no gate in that file had ever read a line of it. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** Both gates now read the markdown plus a named list of published HTML, and a new partition test holds that list to what `docs/` actually publishes, so a second page joining the site is announced rather than governed by nothing. Measured, each mutant alone: the landing page’s badge restated as the previous Graph major, and a `graph.`-family upload host the allowlist does not admit written into the same page, each survived all 1939 tests before the repair and fail after it. The same stale pin planted in `docs/stability.md` was killed on both sides — the positive control that the version gate was live all along on the half it could see. An unpoliced second page, `docs/pricing.html`, survived before the repair and is named by the partition test after it. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-161 | An audit report says a gate’s hole “survives the entire suite”. That is a claim about every test in the repository, and the evidence behind it is usually a grep for the assertion that would have caught it. A grep knows one spelling. Three of seven findings in a single batch were false for that reason alone: the pin existed under a spelling the search did not know — `deepEqual(clamped.calls[0], {` and `deepEqual(calls, [` where it looked for `deepEqual(calls[0], {` — or lived in a sibling test named for something else entirely. | Run the mutant before repairing it: in a snapshot, against the whole suite, and record the kill site by name. The cost of skipping that step is not zero. A repair written against a refuted premise states a false reason in a comment that will outlive the repair, and every assertion added for a hole that was never open dilutes the next real finding. Where such a repair is worth keeping anyway — a pin made self-sufficient rather than a hole closed — the comment says which of the two it is. See [[CC-PROC-109]] and [[CC-PROC-112]]. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-162 | The catalogue opens with a paragraph that describes the catalogue: which section a new row lands in, how many rows that section holds, how many the document defines in all, which foreign prefixes it collects — and, a sentence later, how many places in the repo cite a case. It is the only description of the document’s own shape, and it is where a reader stops. Nothing read it. Measured 2026-09-23: the paragraph put the tail at 94 of 334 rows when it held 258 of 373 — the 94 had never been the section’s size at all but the count of its foreign-prefix rows, and the total was 39 rows behind — and it put the citation surface at 575 when the tree held 613. The same stale 575 had been copied twice into the docstring of the one gate file that exists to be about this document. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** `test/docs-catalog.test.ts` derives every figure the paragraph states — from the section headings, the main-table rows and the same walk the citation gate uses — and compares it with what the paragraph claims, the per-prefix breakdown and the section number included. The sentence is read literally, so a rewrite that drops its shape fails on the first assertion instead of quietly checking nothing. Measured: the old wording restored, the stale citation figure, and one wrong count in the breakdown each survived all 1940 tests before the repair and fail after it; a reworded sentence and a wrong section number fail after it too. A stale Graph version token planted in the same paragraph was killed on both sides — the positive control that the suite had been reading the file all along, just never this paragraph. See [[CC-PROC-153]]. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-163 | The path-injection guard sweeps `src/api/` for request paths that interpolate a value and fails when one belongs to no exercised call. It read the directory with a plain `readdirSync`, and a plain `readdirSync` is not a scope — it is the absence of one. A module one level down comes back as its containing directory’s name, the `.ts` filter drops it, and nothing is reported about what is inside; the floor above the walk counts files, not coverage, so it stays green, while `tsconfig.json` compiles that module into the build all the same. Measured 2026-09-23: a new api module under `src/api/edges/` interpolating a caller-supplied id straight into its request path — no `encodeURIComponent` — passed all 1941 tests. | **Closed 2026-09-23.** The walk is `readdirSync(apiDir, { recursive: true, encoding: 'utf8' })`, the spelling the neighbouring walks in `test/source-citations.test.ts`, `test/release/plugin-manifest.test.ts`, `test/release/mcpb-manifest.test.ts` and `test/release/fixtures.test.ts` already use, and the docstring now states the depth and the extension as decisions and what each one costs when it is left to a default. After the repair the same module fails the guard by name — `edges/reels.ts:getReel` — and the suite is clean once it is removed. | CC-PROC-184 | A response BODY is a stream, and it is still governed by the deadline that carried the headers. So anything that waits between the headers and the read spends that deadline on bytes which have already arrived, and can turn a delivered response into a failure without the server doing anything differently. A proactive throttle is exactly such a wait, and it is the one a client reaches for when it is closest to the limit. | Measured 2026-09-23 in `src/core/http.ts`. On success the loop read usage headers, slept `THROTTLE_MS` (1000) when `maxPct` exceeded `THROTTLE_PCT` (90), and only then called `readBody(res)`. Two things compound there. The throttle sleeps on `opts.signal`, never on the per-attempt `AbortSignal.timeout(settings.timeoutMs)` built at the top of the loop, so it burns its full second however little of the deadline is left (the same unobservable-signal seam as [[CC-PROC-2]]); and the stream it leaves unread is still on that deadline. End-to-end against the real `undici` and the real system clock, one loopback request whose headers land at ~2 ms carrying `x-app-usage` at 95 percent: at `timeoutMs: 30000` it returns at 1009 ms, and at `timeoutMs: 900` the byte-identical response fails at 1004 ms with `DOMException` / `AbortError`. The primitive underneath, on Node 22.23.2 with no repo code in it: under a 300 ms timeout a body resolves at 8 ms with no delay and throws `AbortError` at 602 ms with 600 ms of delay in front of it. `IG_TIMEOUT_MS` is clamped to a floor of 1 (`src/core/settings.ts:307`), so an operator who lowers it below roughly a second plus the round trip makes every throttled call fail its body read; at the 30000 default the same window opens whenever headers take longer than 29 s, which is when a hot account is most likely to be throttled anyway. Second half: both `readBody` call sites sit OUTSIDE the transport `try`/`catch`, so the rejection left the seam as whatever the stream threw - a raw `DOMException` that `isInstagramError` answers false for, with no `kind` for the retry matrix and no shape for the tool layer to render. Repaired by draining the body before the throttle sleep, which costs nothing because the bytes are already on the wire and the throttle exists to delay the NEXT call, and by mapping a stream failure through `toInstagramError` inside `readBody` itself so a third call site cannot reintroduce the hole. Why 1972 tests missed it: `test/core/http.test.ts` says so in its own header - an injected `fetchImpl` returning `new Response(payload)`, whose buffered body can neither fail nor be ordered against anything, and a recording clock whose `sleep` resolves instantly, so no deadline can elapse during a throttle that takes no time. Both doubles had to gain a seam before the defect could be seen at all. | post-M5 · unit |

Positive control against the unrepaired module, both failures being the claim rather than a structural miss: `not ok 53` diffing `+ sleep` ahead of `read`, and `not ok 54` reading `expected an InstagramError, got AbortError: The operation was aborted.`. The one test covering this warning planted mistyped names only, and a mistyped name is warned about from either side of the mutation — so the exact failure the split catalogue exists to prevent had never been observable. An operator who sets the HTTP bearer, points at an explicit env file, or selects a tool package is then told at startup that the name is a typo and that nothing reads it; following that advice deletes a knob that was working. The same test was also the only place the namespace boundary could be seen, and relaxing `startsWith('IG_')` to `startsWith('IG')` survived too — no fixture had ever stood on the far side of that underscore, though an ordinary interactive shell exports `IGNOREEOF`, and the warning names every variable it does not recognise. Closed by extending `an IG_* name nothing reads is warned about by name at startup, values withheld` with one correctly spelt name from each unpinned list and one `IG`-but-not-`IG_` name; the whole-array `deepEqual` already there does the killing. Positive control, each failure being the claim and not a structural miss: `not ok 9` diffing `+ 'IG_ENV_FILE'` and `+ 'IG_HTTP_TOKEN'` into the warned array, `+ 'IG_TOOL_PACKAGES'` for the package list, and `+ 'IGNOREEOF'` for the boundary. See [[CC-CFG-13]] and [[CC-PROC-187]]. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-191 | A candidate list with two entries needs two disproofs. Measured 2026-09-23: appending `path.resolve(process.cwd(), '.env')` back onto the explicit branch SURVIVED the whole 1979-test suite. `override: false` is what makes the defect quiet rather than safe: the explicit file still wins on every key it sets, so credentials do not move, and only the keys it does NOT set stay injectable — from whatever `.env` happens to sit in the directory the MCP client launched the server from, which is the client's choice and not the operator's. A write-mode knob, a timeout, a tool-package selection or a log level picked up that way is invisible in the operator's own env file and in the explicit one they were told replaced everything. Closed by writing a project-local `.env` carrying a single unrecognised name into the sandbox working directory and asserting it never reaches the startup warning — the cheapest observable for "this file was read at all", and one that cannot be mistaken for a credential the test needs for another reason. Positive control: `not ok 21` reading `the project-local .env was read in spite of the explicit path:` above the warn line naming `IG_LEAKED_FROM_PROJECT_ENV`, which is the claim. | CC-INS-14 | A default is only a kindness if it is the default for the thing the caller named. `metrics` and `media_product_type` are independently optional, so the most natural story call — name the product type, let the server choose the metrics — was the one shape the server refused. | **Found and fixed by mutation testing 2026-09-23.** `getMediaInsights` read `params.metrics ?? DEFAULT_MEDIA_METRICS` and then validated the outcome against `MEDIA_METRIC_MATRIX[params.mediaProductType]`, so the two halves disagreed by construction: the fallback is the FEED row, and the FEED row carries `likes`, `comments` and `saved`, none of which are in the STORY row. Every `getMediaInsights({ mediaId, mediaProductType: 'STORY' })` with no explicit `metrics` therefore threw `InstagramError` `kind=validation` listing metrics the caller had never asked for — a refusal manufactured entirely by the server's own default, on the argument shape its own tool description most encourages. The fix reads the matrix row for the type the caller named and keeps the feed set as the fallback for an absent or unrecognised one (`MEDIA_METRIC_MATRIX[type] ?? DEFAULT_MEDIA_METRICS`), so the matrix [[CC-INS-2]] already owns is now read in both directions instead of one — and an unrecognised type still lands on the feed set, which is what [[CC-DATA-6]]'s open vocabulary requires. The description was wrong in the mirror image and was repaired with it: it promised the feed list to a model that had just been told to pass `media_product_type`, so the text was talking the model into the failing call. Why 1979 tests missed it: every media-insights test passed `metrics` explicitly except one, and that one passed no product type either, so the default and the validator were never handed different answers. Closed by `a story with no explicit metrics asks for the story row, not the feed row (CC-INS-14)`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-192 | An equivalent-mutant note is a claim about a program that can be BUILT. Argue one from runtime semantics alone and it can excuse a mutant the type checker would have refused — a note that protects nothing, standing exactly where the next reader looks for a reason not to write the test. | Seven measurements over `api/insights.ts`, `api/discovery.ts` and the insights tool layer, 2026-09-23. **(1)** The retention gate's `params.nowMs !== undefined` arm carried a note arguing that dropping it is unobservable, because `Math.floor(undefined / 1000)` is `NaN` and every `<` against `NaN` is false. True of JavaScript, false of this module: `nowMs` is `number \| undefined`, so the deletion is `error TS18048: 'params.nowMs' is possibly 'undefined'` and never stages. Its stageable neighbour — the arm dropped together with a `?? Date.now()` default — is a real behaviour change, judging a clock-less caller's window against the machine's wall clock, and it SURVIVED all 1979 tests; the note had been standing in for the test that was missing. Closed by `a clock-less call runs no retention logic at all, whatever the epochs say`. **(2)** The mid-millisecond retention fixture read `NOW_MS + 137`, and a remainder below half a second cannot tell `Math.round` from `Math.floor` — so a test whose own comment claims to pin rounding up in general separated `ceil` from `floor` and nothing else. `+ 999` separates all three. **(3)** Three of the four insights readers were asserted member by member, so an extra key added to a returned object went unseen in this file and died only in the tool layer's schema — the borrowed-protection shape of [[CC-PROC-182]], here three times in one module; all three results are now pinned whole, as [[CC-INS-13]] already required of the fourth. **(4)** `getHashtagMedia`'s `res.data ?? []` carried the same loan: a mutant fabricating an item for a non-list `data` passed all 24 tests in its own file and died only on the tool layer's `Array.isArray` fence. The replacement pins the empty page AND the deliberate ABSENCE of normalisation here, by identity, so the next reader can tell "not this layer's job" from "not done". **(5)** `api/discovery.ts`'s header said every call in the file carries the operated account's id as a `user_id` parameter; `discoverBusiness` sends no `user_id` at all and addresses that id as the path node its field expression hangs off, so the one call whose shape a reader is most likely to get wrong was the one the header described wrongly. **(6)** The same file cited `docs/security.md` §7 for the field-expression rule, which lives in §3; §7 is content policy. **(7)** `api/insights.ts` enumerated the §6 rows it owns and stopped two short of the eight that exist, so "not our problem" and "we forgot" were indistinguishable; it now accounts for all eight, including [[CC-INS-4]], which is open here in the strong sense that no timezone conversion happens anywhere in the module. See [[CC-INS-14]]. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-DATA-47 | `fetchPagedEdge` (`src/api/media.ts`) published the page cursor whenever the item cap stopped the walk — including when the cap stopped part-way THROUGH a page. A Graph cursor addresses a page boundary, never an offset inside a page, so that cursor resumes past the items the cap had just discarded: a caller who follows it reads a hole nothing in the response mentions, which is the silent truncation [[CC-DATA-4]] exists to prevent. Reachable on the stock config — `IG_MAX_ITEMS` defaults to 200 and any caller `limit` that does not divide it (`limit: 30`) cuts a page mid-way, while `IG_MAX_ITEMS=0`, the documented "freeze reads" setting, hands back a cursor past an entire unread page. Measured on a five-item page with `maxItems: 3`: `listMedia` returned items one to three carrying an `after` that points past item five, in BOTH the single-page and the `fetchAll` shape, while `getHashtagMedia` (`src/api/discovery.ts`) — asked the identical question on identical input — withheld the cursor and said so in its own docstring. Two modules, one question, opposite answers, each documenting its answer as the contract. `listComments` shares this walk, so the comments listing carried the same hole. | **Fixed, not documented** — three of this module's own promises already decided it: `PagedResult`'s docstring calls `truncated: true` with no `after` a real and meaningful combination ([[CC-DATA-11]]), `fetchPagedEdge`'s own docstring calls a short listing with neither a cursor nor an explanation the silent drop the contract promises will not happen, and `api/discovery.ts` already implemented this exact rule in these exact words. Both cap sites now publish `after` only when the cap landed ON a page boundary; a cap that cut a page short withholds it and carries a shared `CAP_MID_PAGE_NOTE` instead. The note speaks of the ITEMS dropped and not of a cursor, because the last-page shape reaches it with no cursor to speak of at all — `paging: {}` — and a note that named one would be false there. The `fetchAll` cap became a three-way so that every branch stays reachable under the hundred-percent branch gate, and the unusable-cursor stop ([[CC-DATA-11]]) keeps its own distinct note. Six tests that asserted the defect were corrected to the contract; the whole-sentence note table ([[CC-DATA-29]]) grew a seventh case and its promise-versus-result check. Closed by `a capped read never hands back a cursor that resumes past what it dropped (CC-DATA-47)`, which states the guarantee as a round trip rather than as a shape: follow whatever cursor the walk publishes and the next item must be the first one not yet seen, and a withheld cursor must instead carry a reason. Positive control: four hand mutants, four killed — republishing the cursor in either cap site is killed by the new round-trip test and by the renamed cap tests, swapping in the unusable-cursor note is killed by the note table. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-48 | A tool layer that republishes a CAST wire body against a TYPED output schema has handed the third party a veto over the whole call. `hashtagMediaOutput` declares `media_type`, `media_url`, `permalink` and `timestamp` as strings and `like_count` and `comments_count` as numbers, but `api/discovery.ts` casts the body it parses, so one `like_count: null` in a page of fifty is not a degraded row — it is `MCP error -32602` and the other forty-nine rows never reach the caller. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** `mediaToRecord` fenced `caption` and trusted the six scalars beside it, and `businessToRecord` spread a nested `media.data` entry through the same unchecked path, so a mistyped field inside a discovered profile took the profile down with it. Both now re-check every scalar the schema declares and DELETE the ones that fail rather than coercing them: an absent optional field is something a caller can reason about, a coerced one is a fabrication, and deleting the field keeps the row and its id. Additive keys Meta has not announced still ride along untouched, which is what [[CC-DATA-7]] requires of every passthrough shape here. Closed by `every scalar the hashtag-media schema declares is re-checked, not trusted (CC-DATA-48)` and `a discovered profile keeps its usable half when a nested media entry is mistyped (CC-DATA-48)`. Positive control: six mutants, one per re-checked scalar, six killed in lane, and a comment-only negative control that survived. See [[CC-INS-15]] and [[CC-DATA-23]]. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-INS-15 | The same veto, one layer over. All four insights tools publish `metrics` against `insightMetricOutput`, whose `name` is REQUIRED and whose `values` must be a list of objects, and `api/insights.ts` casts the body those rows come from. A row Meta typed loosely, a row with no `name`, a `data` that arrived as an object instead of a list, or a `paging` that arrived null, each failed structured-output validation and turned twenty perfectly good metrics into a protocol error. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** A shared `publishable` step now stands between every one of the four results and `json()`: rows that are not objects or cannot be named are dropped, the declared scalars on the rows that survive are re-checked and deleted rather than coerced, `values` is FILTERED to its object members instead of being discarded whole, a non-list `data` becomes an empty page, and a `paging` that is not an object is removed — removed, not invented, so a result that never carried one still does not. `window`, `notes`, `mediaId`, `breakdown` and `timeframe` are computed from already-validated inputs and are left alone, which is the whole reason the repair is scoped to two fields. Closed by `a metric row Meta typed loosely costs the caller that field, not the call (CC-INS-15)` and the two tests beside it. Positive control: ten mutants over the new guards, ten killed in lane. See [[CC-DATA-48]] and [[CC-INS-13]]. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-193 | An equivalent-mutant note that names the WRONG half of an expression is worse than no note: it tells the next reader that the part actually doing the work is incidental, and it stands exactly where the test that would have caught the swap belongs. Two such notes, both plausible, both written by someone reasoning about the code rather than running it. | Two exhaustive measurements, 2026-09-23. **(1)** `normalizeHashtag` strips `[\s#]+` and then trims, and its comment argued the ORDER of the two calls was load-bearing. It is not: the class has already eaten the leading whitespace by the time the trim runs. Over every string of length zero to five drawn from an alphabet of nine characters — 66430 inputs — the swapped order differs on 0 of them, while narrowing the class to `^#+` differs on 4062, so what is actually load-bearing is the `\s` INSIDE the class. The comment now pins the class and says so with the counts. **(2)** `discover_business` computes `args.mediaLimit ?? Math.min(25, maxItems)` and then clamps the result to `maxItems`, and the inner `Math.min` is dead arithmetic — measured across `maxItems` one to a thousand by `mediaLimit` absent or zero to a hundred, 102000 pairs, 0 differences. The note kept it anyway, because the input description promises the reader that very expression, and states the other half in the same breath: dropping the OUTER clamp differs on 4950 of those same pairs, so exactly one of the two is removable and the note now says which. Rule: a note claiming an alternative spelling is unobservable is a claim about a program that can be BUILT and RUN, and it carries the input space and the counts, or it is a guess. See [[CC-PROC-192]] and [[CC-PROC-182]]. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-AUTH-45 | An expiry slot on the `debug_token` payload holds a value that is not a number at all — `null`, `false`, `"0"`, `"1800000000"`, `[]`, `[1800000000]` — because `req` CASTS the body into its wire type instead of validating it. Every numeric guard waves these through: the product `x * 1000` coerces to a finite number, so the NaN and range clauses never fire. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** Measured the same day: each of those inputs rendered a confident instant — `null` and `"0"` as the 1970 epoch, so `token_status` and `doctor` reported a token of unknown standing as long expired, and a data-access window as closed. `isoFromEpochSeconds` (`src/core/time.ts`) now asks `typeof` first and answers `undefined` for anything that is not a number; `summarizeTokenExpiry` and `expiryLabel` then report the state as `unknown` and name the offending value through `describeWireValue`, which tags a non-number with its type (`"0" (type string)`) so the warning never shows an empty value or a string that reads as a valid number. The never-expires sentinel is compared strictly, so `"0"` and `false` are not read as "never". Closed by `isoFromEpochSeconds answers undefined for …` (ten rows), `summarizeTokenExpiry: … in the expiry slot degrades to unknown, not a RangeError` (ten rows), `expiryLabel does not read a non-number zero as the never-expires sentinel` and `token_status treats a null data-access expiry as unknown, not as absent or as 1970`. Positive controls: `!== undefined` relaxed to truthiness in the tool, and each arm of `describeWireValue`, are all KILLED. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-194 | Two copies of the same "is this a representable instant" guard, one per layer, each with its own equivalent-mutant notes, are two places for the next fix to be applied once. The copy in `src/api/account.ts` existed on the claim that the API layer must not depend on `src/core/time.ts` — a claim the layering rules never made (`api` may import `core`; `src/api/publishing.ts` already imports `core/clock.js`). | Found 2026-09-23 while closing [[CC-AUTH-45]], which was the drift [[CC-PROC-165]] predicts: the non-number hole had to be closed twice, and the notes guarding each copy disagreed about which rewrites were safe. `summarizeTokenExpiry` now delegates to `isoFromEpochSeconds`, the private `MAX_TIMESTAMP_MS` copy is gone, and the module header no longer states the false dependency rule. One consequence was measured rather than assumed: with the `typeof` clause in place, testing `epochSec` instead of the product for `NaN` is now a genuine equivalent (the mutant survives the whole suite), and the note beside the guard says so and says it rests on the `typeof` clause. Rule: before keeping a duplicate for a layering reason, read the lint rule that enforces the layering. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-PROC-195 | Three equivalent-mutant notes — `instagram_list_tagged_media`, `igIdOf` in `src/tools/publishing.ts` and the reachability check in `src/cli/doctor.ts` — declared `accountId ?? 'me'` indistinguishable from `\|\| 'me'` because `clean()` keeps a blank id out of every PARSED profile, while three sibling tools already pinned the same line with a hand-built `accountId: ''`. The same argument was retracted in one file and kept in three. | Found 2026-09-23. With `\|\|`, a blank configured id falls through to the token owner's `/me` and the call succeeds against the wrong account: tagged media and the publishing quota are read for someone other than the configured account, and `doctor` reports reachability it never checked. Measured the same day: at each of the three sites the `\|\|` spelling survived the whole suite. Each now has a test with `accountId: ''` that asserts the blank id is KEPT (`//tags`, `//content_publishing_limit`, `GET /`) — the blank-id test beside the `/me/tags` fallback test in `test/tools/comments.test.ts`, `get_publishing_limit does not coerce a blank configured account id into /me`, `reachability does not coerce a blank configured account id into /me` — and each mutant is KILLED by it. Rule: an unreachability argument that the parser guarantees is about PARSED input; a seam that also takes hand-built input is not covered by it. See [[CC-PROC-165]]. | post-M5 · process |
| CC-AUTH-46 | A token glued to an identifier that merely CONTAINS `IG_` — `CONFIG_IGQ…`, `SIG_IGQ…`, `ORIG_TOKEN-IGQ…` — reaches an error message, a log line or a tool result. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The `IG` shape backstop in `core/redact.ts`, and its copy in `core/errors.ts`, exempted any run with `IG_` ANYWHERE behind the match, while its comment and [[CC-PROC-186]] promise the exemption only when `IG_` STARTS the run. Measured the same day: all five glued inputs passed through both copies unmasked, whole. The lookbehind is now anchored — `IG_` must not itself follow an identifier character — and sits after the third character of the match, which the fuzz run showed gives identical output and removes a quadratic slowdown the anchored-first spelling had on adversarial input. Own names (`IG_PROFILE_DIGITALSTORE_ACCESS_TOKEN`, bare, quoted, after `=`) stay verbatim. Closed by `the IG_ exemption holds only when IG_ starts the run, not wherever it appears in it`, `the IG_ exemption spans a profile slug of any length and alphabet` and `the IG_ exemption needs IG_ to start the run, and then spans any slug`; with the old pattern restored, the new tests fail. Residual, inherent: a token glued with `_` directly after one of our own names cannot be told from the name. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-RATE-16 | A caller cancels a Graph call (its `AbortSignal` is already aborted, or aborts while the call waits for a per-host concurrency slot) while `maxConcurrent` is exhausted. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The per-host semaphore in `core/http.ts` never looked at the signal. Measured with a fake fetch at `maxConcurrent: 1`: an already-aborted call and a call aborted mid-wait both stayed pending for as long as the slot was held, then TOOK the freed slot and handed `fetch` a dead signal — in production up to four timed-out attempts plus backoff later, at the expense of a live request's place. Now an already-aborted signal rejects before any slot or socket is spent, an abort while queued removes the waiter (without touching the active count) and rejects at once, and a granted waiter detaches its listener, so a long-lived caller signal accumulates none. Closed by `an already-aborted signal settles before any fetch, even with a slot free`, `an already-aborted call does not queue behind a busy slot`, `aborting a queued call rejects it at once and gives its place to the next waiter` and `a queued call that is granted its slot leaves no listener on the caller signal`; all four fail against the old semaphore. The same audit corrected `IG_TIMEOUT_MS` in `.env.example` and the docs: it bounds one HTTP attempt, not a call, since retries and their backoff or `Retry-After` waits are outside it. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-47 | An untyped caller (a `.mjs` script) passes a token string as the logger's `fields`, or as `child()` bindings. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** Both were spread into the record BEFORE redaction, so a string became one key per character; no single character matches a token shape or a registered secret, and the token was written to the line in pieces, trivially reassembled. `core/log.ts` now carries a primitive or boxed string whole under the key `fields`, so the redactor sees the full value; objects, arrays, `null` and `undefined` pass through unchanged. Closed by `a string passed as fields is redacted whole, never spread one character per key` and `string child bindings are redacted whole, never spread one character per key`. **Amended 2026-09-26:** a boxed string NESTED inside a field value had the same char-split leak in the redactor itself, closed by CC-DATA-122. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-48 | A logged or journalled value carries an own `toJSON` function that returns a token, or uses a token as an object key. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The redactor copied functions through and never looked at keys, so `JSON.stringify` called the copied `toJSON` AFTER redaction and wrote the raw token, and a token key was written verbatim. `core/redact.ts` now drops an own `toJSON` function from the clone (the clone serialises from its redacted fields) and masks keys like values; a non-function `toJSON` field is copied as usual, and function values elsewhere keep their pass-through. Known limit: a function value that itself carries an own `toJSON` is still passed by reference. Closed by `an own toJSON function is dropped, so serialising the clone cannot write an unmasked token` and `a token used as an object key is masked in the key`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-49 | A write's summary, details or target id carries a secret that runs past the confirmation prompt's length cap (200 per field, 800 for the details blob, 64 for the target id). | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** `mcp/write-mode.ts` capped each field first and redacted the assembled prompt after, and both redaction passes match whole values only, so a secret cut by the cap left a prefix neither pass recognised — measured: 20 hex digits of a fixture app secret, and the first 64 characters of a token-shaped target id, printed in the elicitation prompt. Every field, and the target id, is now scrubbed (profile credentials, then the global registry and token shapes) BEFORE it is capped; the assembled message keeps its final pass. Closed by `prompt: a secret straddling the length cap is masked, not cut into a readable prefix`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-49 | The confirmation prompt's length cap lands between the two halves of a surrogate pair (an emoji in a caption). | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The cap counts UTF-16 code units, so it could leave a lone high surrogate — a `\p{C}` character, the class `sanitizeLine` exists to strip — at the cut. The orphan is now dropped before the ellipsis. Same failure class as [[CC-DATA-3]], at a different cap. Closed by `prompt: a length cap never splits a surrogate pair into a lone surrogate`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PROC-196 | A web page served on port 80 of the bound address (`Origin: http://127.0.0.1`) POSTs to the HTTP listener on another port. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The `Origin` allowlist was built by prefixing `http://` onto the `Host` allowlist, which deliberately carries port-less forms ([[CC-PROC-32]]); as an origin a port-less value names the port-80 site, so it was admitted (200) while `http://127.0.0.1:<port + 1>` was refused (403). Low severity — the listener sends no `Access-Control-*` header, so a browser preflight still fails — but the `Origin` check is the layer that does not rely on that. `allowedOrigins` in `mcp/transport.ts` now lists exactly one serialised origin per bound name at the bound port. Sibling of [[CC-PROC-63]]. Closed by `the Origin allowlist names each bound name at the bound port and nothing port-less` and `a cross-origin browser POST is refused even though its Host is the bound address`. | post-M5 · unit/process |
| CC-PROC-197 | A reversible write tool omits `destructiveHint`. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The MCP spec defaults an absent `destructiveHint` to true on a tool that is not read-only, so five comment tools (reply, create, hide, unhide, set commenting) were advertised as destructive, and the comment test's `notEqual(…, true)` passed on the absent value. They now declare `false`, and the metadata contract derives the expected value from the gate — `withWriteGate` reads `allowDestructive` only for a destructive intent — instead of a list of names, as [[CC-PROC-8]] does for `readOnlyHint`. Closed by `every write tool declares destructiveHint, and it matches what the gate is asked`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-46 | The publishing-limit row carries a `quota_total` or `quota_duration` that is not a finite number: JSON `null`, a numeric string, or an out-of-range literal that parses to Infinity. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** A `null` total made `remaining` equal `Math.max(0, null - usage)`, which is 0. That told the caller the quota was exhausted on an account that may have its whole allowance left. A `null` in either field also failed the tool's numeric output schema, and a string total was coerced into a `remaining` count and returned as a string. Any value that is not a finite number is now reported as unknown (absent), and `remaining` is derived only from a known total. Closed by `getPublishingLimit treats a non-numeric quota total or window as unknown, never as zero remaining`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-47 | A container-status read returns `status_code` or `status` as something other than a string: JSON `null` or a number. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The body is cast, not validated, so the value was passed through. A `null` failed the tool's string output schema. A number such as `0` crashed the publish flow with a raw TypeError from `.toUpperCase()` instead of the typed unknown-status error that its deadline exists to give. A non-string code or detail is now reported as absent. The flow keeps polling and ends with the unknown-status error, and nothing is published. Closed by `getContainerStatus reports a non-string code or detail as absent rather than passing it through` and `runPublishFlow answers a non-string status_code with the unknown-status error, not a TypeError`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-50 | A paged edge hands back a cursor that the walk already followed, but not on the very next page: a cycle `A → B → A`, or a return to the caller's own starting `after`. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The repeat guard compared each cursor only with the previous one, so it caught `A → A` and missed longer cycles. The walk re-read pages already collected until the item cap. The listing then held duplicate items and, when the cap was what stopped it, no note. The walk now remembers every cursor it has sent, seeded with the caller's `after`. It stops on any repeat with the existing same-cursor note and returns that cursor as the resume point. The media and comments listings share this walk. Closed by `listMedia fetchAll stops when the edge cycles back to a cursor it already followed` and ``a walk resumed from `after` stops when the edge cycles back to that starting cursor``. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-50 | `login` meets a stray token right after a value-taking flag that was itself swallowed as the previous flag's value (`--app-id --app-secret <secret>`) or given an empty inline value (`--app-secret= <secret>`). | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The leftover token is the value the operator meant for that flag, which in the case that matters is the app secret. The unknown-argument refusal echoed it whole to stderr. The refusal now names the flag the token followed, never prints the token, and advises the inline form. For `-p` it advises `--path=`, because a short flag takes no inline value. A token after a flag that already has its value is still echoed as before. Closed by `a refusal never echoes a token that follows a value-taking flag`, `no value-taking spelling lets the token after it be echoed, and the advice names a form the parser takes` and `an unknown word that does not follow a value-taking flag is still echoed whole`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-AUTH-51 | `doctor` reads a `debug_token` payload with no `is_valid` field. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The verdict was "fail when false, otherwise OK", so a missing field printed `OK Token is valid` with no evidence behind it. The line is now OK only for `true`, FAIL for `false`, and INFO ("not reported") when the field is absent, which points at the reachability check. The exit code is unchanged, because an omitted field is not a failure ([[CC-DATA-2]]). Closed by `a debug_token payload without is_valid is not reported as a valid token either` and `a debug_token payload without is_valid is not reported as an invalid token`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-38 | `login --profile` is given a name with a character outside `[a-z0-9_.-]` after lowercasing (a space, `=`, an accented letter). | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** dotenv parses only keys made of `[\w.-]`, so `IG_PROFILE_<NAME>_*` with any other character was written to the credentials file, reported as stored with exit 0, and then silently dropped on load. The credentials were unreachable. Such a name is now refused with exit 2 before any network call, and nothing is written. Closed by `a profile name the env file cannot hold is refused before any network call` and `a profile name of accepted characters is stored and loads back under that name`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-39 | The credential store is written for a profile whose name holds `-` or `.` (allowed by [[CC-AUTH-18]]), or a name the env scheme cannot read back (a space, `=`, `straße`), and `writeCredentials` is reached without going through `login`. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The merge recognised only POSIX-identifier keys, so a line like `IG_PROFILE_SECOND-STUDIO_ACCESS_TOKEN=…` was never matched. Every `login` or `refresh` then appended a fresh assignment and left the superseded token on disk in plaintext. The key class is now dotenv's own `[\w.-]`, so such a line is updated in place. The writer also refuses a name outside `[a-z0-9_.-]` after lowercasing, before anything is written. Such a name either produced a key dotenv never reads, or case-folded into a different profile (`straße` came back as `strasse`). This is the core-level counterpart of the CLI check in [[CC-CFG-38]]. Closed by `a hyphenated or dotted profile is updated in place, no stale token left` and `a profile name that cannot read back is refused, and nothing is written`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-40 | Two writers update the credential store at once: a `login` for one profile and a `refresh` of another, or two server windows refreshing together ([[CC-AUTH-14]]). | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** Each write is atomic, because it goes through a temp file and a rename, but the read-merge-write sequence was not. Both writers read the same old text, and the second rename silently discarded the first writer's credentials. Writes now hold an exclusive sibling lock, `<store>.lock`, created with `wx` at 0600, and re-read the store under it. A waiter retries every 20 ms and gives up after 10 s with a validation error that names the lock. A lock older than 30 s, or a dangling link, counts as left by a crashed writer and is cleared. Any create error other than "exists" fails at once, wrapped like every other store step. Accepted residual: two waiters that judge the same stale lock at the same instant can both clear it. That window opens only after a writer has crashed. Closed by `two concurrent writes for different profiles both survive`, `a held lock is waited on, then times out naming the lock, store untouched`, `a writer blocked on a lock proceeds once the holder releases it`, `a stale lock from a crashed writer is cleared, not left to wedge logins`, `a dangling lock link, which nothing could ever release, is cleared as stale` and `a lock that waiting cannot help create fails at once, wrapped`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-CFG-41 | An MCP client passes `IG_AUTH_MODE` to pin the auth path, and the env file already holds the `IG_AUTH_PATH` that `login` or `refresh` stored. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The path resolves as `IG_AUTH_PATH ?? IG_AUTH_MODE`, and the client-over-file rule applies variable by variable, so the stored `IG_AUTH_PATH` wins. The setup guide said to "set `IG_AUTH_MODE` explicitly to override", which stops being true once a store exists. The precedence is kept deliberately: flipping it would let a stale `IG_AUTH_MODE` in the file beat what `login` just stored. The setup guide, the auth guide and the architecture document now say that a client pins the path with `IG_AUTH_PATH`. Closed by `an IG_AUTH_MODE from the client loses to the IG_AUTH_PATH login stores, as the guides say`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-51 | `instagram_list_media` or `instagram_get_media` receives a field Meta typed loosely: `caption: null`, a `null` count or URL, an item or carousel child with no `id`, or a `children.data` that is not a list. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** `api/media` casts the body, so a `null` caption reached `fence()` and threw a TypeError that the registry rendered as an `upstream` error, and any other mistyped scalar failed output validation, which cost the caller the whole listing as `MCP error -32602`. Each declared field is now kept only when it has its declared type, and an entry with no usable id is dropped, the rule `tools/discovery` already applied. Closed by `real McpServer: a loosely typed field costs the caller that field, not the listing (CC-DATA-48)` and `real McpServer: get_media survives a null caption and drops unusable carousel children`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-DATA-52 | `instagram_get_account`, `instagram_list_linked_accounts` or `instagram_token_status` receives a `null` or mistyped profile, page or `debug_token` field. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** `fenceOptional` skipped only `undefined`, so a `null` name threw inside `fence()`. Counts, the picture URL, page and IG ids, `is_valid` and `scopes` were published unchecked, so one `null` failed output validation for the whole result. A value of the wrong type is now reported as absent, and `scopes` keeps only its string entries. Closed by `real McpServer: a null profile field is reported absent, not a crash or a failed call` and `real McpServer: token_status survives a loosely typed debug_token payload`. | post-M5 · unit |
| CC-PUB-48 | `content_publishing_limit` returns a `quota_usage` that is not a finite number: a string, a boolean or an object. | **Found by audit 2026-09-23.** The field was cast as a number, so the tool derived `remaining: NaN` and the required `quotaUsage` failed output validation as `MCP error -32602`. Reporting it as 0 would claim an allowance nobody measured, so it is now refused as an `upstream` error that describes the value. An absent or `null` usage still reads as 0. Closed by `getPublishingLimit refuses a quota_usage that is not a number instead of publishing it`. | post-M5 · unit |
